Section <a href="#static-sec:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-sec:scalar">3</a> develops the scalar evolution and the variational identities of a minimizing measure. Section <a href="#static-sec:locking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-sec:locking">4</a> uses these identities to compare three replicas: when one pair has a substantial positive overlap, their small signed overlaps with the third replica are forced to remain close, apart from a set of very small averaged Gibbs probability. Section <a href="#sec:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:energy">5</a> proves an equilibrium energy bound and a quantitative estimate for small Gaussian rotations of the disorder. All of these specialized inputs are proved within the paper.

There are two further static steps. First, the energy and rotation bounds imply a coverage statement in Section <a href="#sec:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:coverage">6</a>: simultaneously for every subset $S$, outside a set of targets of absolute Gibbs mass less than $e^{-D_1L}$, each $x\in S$ has Gibbs mass at least $e^{-D_3L}$ of $z\in S$ with $|R(x,z)|\ge q_*$. Here $D_3$ is fixed after $D_1$. Sampling $\exp(O(L))$ independent Gibbs configurations can therefore supply a reference inside $S$. Second, Section <a href="#sec:narrow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:narrow">7</a> treats triples whose two smaller overlaps lie near a positive level $r$, while the larger overlap exceeds $r$ by only a shrinking amount. It supplies locking at the scales required by the backwards searches. The estimate uses a scalar stationarity identity on a fixed interval; if that identity fails, a pair-overlap pressure gap supplies a forbidden interval instead. This alternative avoids assuming a complete description of the minimizing measure’s support.

Section <a href="#sec:dynamics" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:dynamics">8</a> applies these estimates to a stationary discrete path $(x_k)$, and obtains the continuous-time path by an independent rate-$n$ Poisson clock. At a crossing of a small positive overlap level, coverage supplies a new reference with large overlap with the current configuration. Searching backwards for the new reference’s crossing, the locking estimates keep the overlaps with previously retained references almost unchanged. Iterating creates many simultaneous overlap constraints at a single path index.

The reference samples are arranged in blocks of a fixed length $B$. Within a block, each new reference is chosen in the set of configurations having small absolute overlap with all the earlier references in that block. At the start of the next block this restriction is reset. A conflict with older retained references can then delete only a suffix of length at most a fixed $H_0<B$. Since $H_0<B$, deletion cannot reach the first reference of the preceding block or any older retained reference. In particular, $v_1$ survives; Figure <a href="#fig:protected-block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:protected-block">2</a> shows this invariant. This construction permits $p$ levels of order $L$ with only $\exp(O(L))$ total reference samples, while retaining a fixed positive fraction of the levels.

The final probability estimate uses the order in which randomness is revealed. At level $i$, the reference is already determined when the independent threshold $r_i$ is drawn uniformly from an interval of length $\rho$. Its overlap at any fixed path index is unlikely to fall in the much narrower permitted window. Removing constraints by conditional expectation in decreasing order of level bounds their joint probability, even though later reference choices depend on earlier thresholds. We sum over deterministic path indices and deterministic subsets of retained levels. This avoids conditioning on the adaptively chosen retained list and completes both clock versions.

Two auxiliary conclusions have a separate role. Appendix <a href="#poly-sec:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly-sec:coverage">9</a> proves that every disorder-selected set of Gibbs mass at least $n^{-D_1}$ has polynomially large conditional mass of pairs with absolute overlap at least a fixed $q_*(\beta)$; this threshold is chosen before $D_1$, and the resulting exponent $D_2$ is fixed before $n$ grows. Its Gaussian-gain lemma and cell corollary are proved in full. Appendix <a href="#fixed-sec:crossings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fixed-sec:crossings">10</a> combines that result with the fixed-scale locking estimate to obtain a second proof of the polynomial-time obstruction. There the number of levels is fixed and the reference banks have polynomial sizes. These appendices reuse the energy, rotation and static locking proofs already established in the body, while retaining their own complete coverage and crossing arguments.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Notation.

We suppress $J$, $n$, and the fixed $\beta$ from $H_n^J$, $\pi_{n,\beta}^J$, and $Z_{n,\beta}^J$ when harmless. All constants may depend on $\beta$ and on other parameters fixed before $n$ grows. Gibbs replicas and reference samples are independent conditional on the disorder unless stated otherwise. For matrices, $U:V=\sum_{a,b}U_{ab}V_{ab}$ and $|U|^2=U:U$ denote the Frobenius inner product and squared norm.

<a id="static-sec:gaussian"></a>
### Gaussian hierarchies and quantitative free energy comparison

The overlap estimates in the later sections require two pressure comparisons: an upper bound with prescribed replica overlaps, and a lower bound for the unconstrained pressure with a controlled finite-size error. We derive both from finite Gaussian hierarchies. The upper bound allows the hierarchy’s covariance endpoint to differ from the prescribed overlap matrix; the cost of that difference remains explicit. For the lower bound, we choose the hierarchy along an overlap-dependent differential equation and estimate its error before making that choice.

The constrained comparison belongs to Guerra’s Gaussian interpolation framework <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Guerra03](#ref-Guerra03)], including Talagrand’s bounds for coupled replicas <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Talagrand07](#ref-Talagrand07), Propositions 3.4 and 4.4] and Panchenko’s vector-spin formulation <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Panchenko18](#ref-Panchenko18)]. Talagrand’s bounds already retain a cost when prescribed overlaps differ from the covariance endpoint. We derive the finite-hierarchy matrix form needed here directly, allowing nonunit endpoint diagonals as well. The quantitative lower comparison is proved separately below. We fix $\beta>0$ throughout. A constant $C_\beta$ may vary between occurrences, but never depends on $n$.

<a id="static-eq:hamiltonian-covariance"></a>
We first remove a constant term from the covariance calculation. Add independent standard Gaussian variables $J_{11},\ldots,J_{nn}$ to the disorder and, only in free-energy calculations, use the Hamiltonian $$\widehat H_n(\sigma)
 =H_n(\sigma)+\frac1{\sqrt{2n}}\sum_{i=1}^nJ_{ii}.$$ The extra term has mean zero and is the same for every $\sigma$. The Gibbs measure and the expected log partition function are therefore unchanged. The covariance now takes the exact form $$\label{static-eq:hamiltonian-covariance}
 \mathbb E\widehat H_n(\sigma)\widehat H_n(\tau)
 =\frac n2R(\sigma,\tau)^2.$$

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Two Gaussian tools and a hierarchy identity

We will need concentration for Gaussian disorder and a variance estimate after conditioning on a spin configuration. The following standard Gaussian and Brascamp–Lieb inequalities supply these two estimates.

<a id="stmt-static-lem-gaussian-tools"></a>
<div id="static-lem:gaussian-tools" class="lemma">

<a id="static-eq:gaussian-concentration"></a>
<a id="static-eq:gaussian-mgf"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Gaussian tools). *Let $G$ be a standard Gaussian vector in a finite-dimensional Euclidean space. If $f$ is $L$-Lipschitz, then for $a>0$ and $\theta\in\mathbb R$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P\bigl[|f(G)-\mathbb Ef(G)|\ge a\bigr]
 &\le 2\exp\left(-\frac{a^2}{2L^2}\right),
 \label{static-eq:gaussian-concentration}\\
 \log\mathbb E\exp\{\theta(f(G)-\mathbb Ef(G))\}
 &\le\frac{\theta^2L^2}{2}.
 \label{static-eq:gaussian-mgf}
\end{aligned}$$ If a probability density on $\mathbb R^m$ is proportional to $e^{-V}$, where $V$ is twice differentiable and $\nabla^2V\succeq I$, then <a id="static-eq:brascamp-lieb"></a>$$\label{static-eq:brascamp-lieb}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(f)\le\mathbb E\|\nabla f\|^2.$$ In particular, the variance of a linear functional $a\cdot G$ under this density is at most $\|a\|^2$.*

</div>

The variance bound is the strongly log-concave case of the Brascamp–Lieb inequality <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[BL76](#ref-BL76)]. For clarity, we prove the concentration estimate and the specialization of the variance bound to linear functions, which is the only specialization used below.

<details id="proof-stmt-static-lem-gaussian-tools"><summary>Proof of stmt-static-lem-gaussian-tools</summary>

*Proof of the needed Gaussian tools.* For the concentration estimate, write $H_s$ for convolution with a centered Gaussian of covariance $sI$, and let $B_t$ be standard Brownian motion. The heat equation and Itô’s formula represent a smooth $L$-Lipschitz function as $$f(B_1)-\mathbb Ef(B_1)
 =\int_0^1\nabla H_{1-t}f(B_t)\cdot\,\mathrm dB_t,
 \qquad |\nabla H_s f|\le L.$$ The integrand is bounded, so its exponential martingale has expectation one. Its quadratic variation is at most $L^2$; this proves Equation <a href="#static-eq:gaussian-mgf" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:gaussian-mgf">[static-eq:gaussian-mgf]</a> for every real $\theta$. Apply exponential Markov’s inequality and optimize separately for the two tails to obtain Equation <a href="#static-eq:gaussian-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:gaussian-concentration">[static-eq:gaussian-concentration]</a>. Smooth convolution and localization, followed by Gaussian integrability, extend the argument to Lipschitz functions of at most linear growth. Every application here lies in that class.

For the linear variance estimate, we may work with the potentials that occur in the proof. They are smooth and satisfy $I\preceq\nabla^2V\preceq CI$, where the finite constant $C$ may depend on the fixed finite system. To see the upper bound, note that the nonquadratic terms are finite logarithmic partition functions or hierarchy prefix values: their Hessians are bounded sums of covariances. A linear tilt leaves the Hessian unchanged. Consider the diffusion $$\,\mathrm dY_t=\sqrt2\,\,\mathrm dB_t-\nabla V(Y_t)\,\mathrm dt$$ and denote its semigroup by $Q_t$. The drift is globally Lipschitz, which gives a unique solution. Under synchronous coupling, strong convexity contracts distances by $e^{-t}$. Thus an $L$-Lipschitz function becomes $e^{-t}L$-Lipschitz under $Q_t$. Integration by parts shows that $\nu(\,\mathrm dy)\propto e^{-V(y)}\,\mathrm dy$ is invariant and that $$\frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm dt}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\nu(Q_tf)
 =-2\int|\nabla Q_tf|^2\,\mathrm d\nu.$$ The Gaussian tails supplied by strong convexity justify the same identity for $f(y)=a\cdot y$ by a cutoff argument. For independent $Y,Y'$ with law $\nu$, contraction also gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\nu(Q_tf)
 \le\tfrac12 e^{-2t}|a|^2\mathbb E|Y-Y'|^2\longrightarrow0.$$ We can therefore integrate the dissipation identity to infinity. The gradient bound $|\nabla Q_tf|\le e^{-t}|a|$ yields $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_\nu(a\cdot Y)\le2|a|^2\int_0^\infty e^{-2t}\,\mathrm dt=|a|^2$. This proves the linear estimate needed for the conditional disorder laws. The finite upper Hessian bound justifies the diffusion argument; at each application we will check its lower Hessian bound explicitly. ◻

</details>

<a id="static-eq:hierarchy-recursion"></a>
We next define the hierarchy and its probability law. Besides any quenched root randomness, take independent Gaussian fields $g_0,\ldots,g_k$. The field $g_0$ belongs to the quenched root. Choose masses $$0=x_{-1}\le x_0\le\cdots\le x_{k-1}\le x_k=1,
 \qquad w_l=x_l-x_{l-1},$$ and a terminal log partition function $F_k$ on a finite configuration space. Integrate the fields backwards by setting, for $1\le j\le k$, $$\label{static-eq:hierarchy-recursion}
 F_{j-1}=\frac1{x_{j-1}}\log\mathbb E_j e^{x_{j-1}F_j}.$$ Here $\mathbb E_j$ integrates the field $g_j$. At a zero mass the right-hand side is interpreted as $\mathbb E_jF_j$. After these recursive integrations, the hierarchy pressure is $\mathbb EF_0$, with expectation also over all quenched randomness.

<a id="static-eq:hierarchy-tilt"></a>
The recursion also specifies a probability law on fields and terminal configurations. Conditional on the prefix through level $j-1$, multiply the original Gaussian law of $g_j$ by the density $$\label{static-eq:hierarchy-tilt}
 W_j=\exp\{x_{j-1}(F_j-F_{j-1})\}.$$ Equation <a href="#static-eq:hierarchy-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:hierarchy-recursion">[static-eq:hierarchy-recursion]</a> normalizes this density. After sampling level $k$, draw a configuration from the terminal Gibbs law. For a pair of paths indexed by a level $l$, sample their root and fields through $l$ once, and then sample the two continuations independently conditional on this prefix. We denote expectation under that pair law by $\mathbb E_l^{(2)}$. At $l=k$ the only independent choices are the terminal thermal draws. More generally, a finite tree is sampled by drawing each shared prefix once and making its descendants conditionally independent. In particular, every branch has the single-path marginal law. This convention fixes the shared randomness in all subsequent integration-by-parts calculations.

<a id="stmt-static-lem-hierarchy-derivative"></a>
<div id="static-lem:hierarchy-derivative" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Hierarchy differentiation). *Suppose an independent standard Gaussian vector $g$ at level $j$ enters the terminal exponent as $\sqrt v\,g\cdot e(S)$, where $e$ is a fixed vector-valued function of the terminal configuration. With all other parameters fixed, <a id="static-eq:hierarchy-derivative"></a>$$\label{static-eq:hierarchy-derivative}
 \frac{\partial}{\partial v}\mathbb EF_0
 =\frac12\left(
 \mathbb E\|e(S)\|^2-
 \sum_{l=j}^k w_l\mathbb E_l^{(2)}e(S^1)\cdot e(S^2)
 \right).$$ A quenched Gaussian is treated as level $0$. The identity holds for $v>0$ and extends to interpolation endpoints by integration and continuity.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-static-lem-hierarchy-derivative"><summary>Proof of stmt-static-lem-hierarchy-derivative</summary>

<a id="static-eq:hessian-recursion-general"></a>
*Proof.* To differentiate the pressure, first add a deterministic vector in the same direction as $\sqrt v\,g$. Differentiating the terminal log partition function and then the hierarchy recursion shows that, for $l\ge j$, the gradient of $F_l$ is the conditional mean $m_l^e$ of $e(S)$ given the prefix through $l$. A second differentiation gives $$\label{static-eq:hessian-recursion-general}
 \nabla^2F_l
 =\mathbb E[\nabla^2F_{l+1}\mid l]
   +x_l\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(m_{l+1}^e\mid l).$$ All conditional expectations in this calculation use the path law. At the terminal level, the Hessian is the thermal covariance of $e(S)$.

For $j\ge1$, differentiate the $j$th logarithmic expectation with respect to the variance and integrate by parts in its Gaussian field. The result is one half of the tilted expectation of $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nabla^2F_j+x_{j-1}\|m_j^e\|^2.$$ At level $j=0$, ordinary Gaussian differentiation omits the second term, exactly as prescribed by $x_{-1}=0$. Earlier levels propagate the derivative by conditional expectation. Iteration of Equation <a href="#static-eq:hessian-recursion-general" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:hessian-recursion-general">[static-eq:hessian-recursion-general]</a> now expresses twice the pressure derivative as $$\mathbb E\|e(S)\|^2-\mathbb E\|m_k^e\|^2
 +\sum_{l=j}^{k-1}x_l
       \bigl(\mathbb E\|m_{l+1}^e\|^2-\mathbb E\|m_l^e\|^2\bigr)
 +x_{j-1}\mathbb E\|m_j^e\|^2.$$ The sum telescopes to $\mathbb E\|e(S)\|^2-\sum_{l=j}^kw_l\mathbb E\|m_l^e\|^2$. Two descendants are independent conditional on their common prefix, so $\mathbb E\|m_l^e\|^2=\mathbb E_l^{(2)}e(S^1)\cdot e(S^2)$. This is the claimed identity.

Gaussian integrability justifies each differentiation: the configuration space and the number of levels are finite, the coupled gradients are bounded, and the recursive values grow at most linearly in the Gaussian variables. A zero mass can be handled directly by the expectation recursion, or obtained as a limit. Integration and continuity give the stated endpoint interpretation. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### A matrix interpolation bound

<a id="static-eq:constrained-pressure"></a>
We now apply the differentiation identity to a prescribed overlap constraint. For $d$ configurations $\sigma^1,\ldots,\sigma^d$, write $D_{ab}=R(\sigma^a,\sigma^b)$ for their Gram matrix. If $D$ is feasible, meaning that at least one such tuple realizes it, define $$\label{static-eq:constrained-pressure}
 F_{d,n}(D)=\frac1n\mathbb E\log
 \sum_{\substack{\sigma^1,\ldots,\sigma^d\in\{-1,1\}^n\\
                  R(\sigma^a,\sigma^b)=D_{ab}\ \forall a,b}}
       \exp\left\{\beta\sum_{a=1}^dH_n(\sigma^a)\right\}.$$ The sum is unnormalized. We use the Frobenius notation $U:V=\sum_{a,b}U_{ab}V_{ab}$ and $|U|^2=U:U$. To compare this pressure with a single-site recursion, choose a symmetric matrix path $$Q_{-1}=0,\qquad Q_j-Q_{j-1}\succeq0\quad(0\le j\le k),
 \qquad Q_k=Q_{\rm end}.$$ Within these conditions, the endpoint is free: it may differ from $D$ and may have nonunit diagonal. Increments of zero covariance are allowed. If the first nonzero increment should carry positive mass, insert a quenched level $Q_0=0$; the first genuine increment is then integrated at mass $x_0$. At a single site, take independent $d$-dimensional Gaussian fields of covariance $\beta^2(Q_j-Q_{j-1})$. For a symmetric multiplier $\lambda$, define $\Psi(Q,x,\lambda)$ as the expected hierarchy value with terminal function $$\log\sum_{s\in\{-1,1\}^d}
     \exp\left\{\left(\sum_{j=0}^kg_j\right)\cdot s
                    +s^{\mathsf T}\lambda s\right\}.$$ For the deterministic correction to the pressure, set <a id="static-eq:matrix-path-integral"></a>$$\label{static-eq:matrix-path-integral}
 \int x\,\,\mathrm d|Q|^2
 =\sum_{j=0}^kx_{j-1}\bigl(|Q_j|^2-|Q_{j-1}|^2\bigr).$$ The initial quenched increment therefore carries mass zero in this integral. The next bound keeps the cost of the free endpoint explicit.

<a id="stmt-static-prop-matrix-bound"></a>
<div id="static-prop:matrix-bound" class="proposition">

<a id="static-eq:matrix-bound"></a>
**Proposition 5** (Matrix interpolation). *For every feasible $D$ and every path and multiplier as above, $$\label{static-eq:matrix-bound}
 F_{d,n}(D)\le
 \Psi(Q,x,\lambda)-\lambda:D
 +\frac{\beta^2}{4}
       \left(|D-Q_{\rm end}|^2-\int x\,\,\mathrm d|Q|^2\right).$$ For fixed $D$ and $\lambda$, the bound passes to any sequence of finite paths for which $Q_{\rm end}$, $\Psi(Q,x,\lambda)$, and the displayed path integral converge.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-static-prop-matrix-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-static-prop-matrix-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Place independent copies of the single-site fields at all $n$ sites. Interpolate from those fields to the common Hamiltonian disorder, retaining the overlap constraint throughout. The terminal exponent is $$\sqrt t\,\beta\sum_{a=1}^d\widehat H_n(\sigma^a)
 +\sqrt{1-t}\sum_{i=1}^n\sum_{j=0}^kg_{j,i}\cdot s_i
 +\sum_{i=1}^ns_i^{\mathsf T}\lambda s_i,
 \qquad s_i=(\sigma_i^1,\ldots,\sigma_i^d).$$ Denote the expected recursive value, divided by $n$, by $\varphi(t)$. For two terminal draws, define the cross-overlap matrix by $R_{ab}=R(\sigma^a,\tau^b)$; this matrix need not be symmetric. Equation <a href="#static-eq:hamiltonian-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:hamiltonian-covariance">[static-eq:hamiltonian-covariance]</a> gives Hamiltonian covariance $n\beta^2|R|^2/2$, whereas the $j$th site-field increment has covariance $n\beta^2(Q_j-Q_{j-1}):R$. The self-overlap is $D$. Sum Lemma <a href="#static-lem:hierarchy-derivative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:hierarchy-derivative">4</a> over these Gaussian increments to obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 \varphi'(t)
 &=\frac{\beta^2}{4}\left[
 |D|^2-2Q_k:D
 -\sum_{l=0}^kw_l\mathbb E_l^{(2)}
       \bigl(|R|^2-2Q_l:R\bigr)\right]\\
 &\le\frac{\beta^2}{4}
       \left(|D|^2-2Q_k:D+\sum_{l=0}^kw_l|Q_l|^2\right).
\end{aligned}$$ The inequality discards the nonnegative squares $|R-Q_l|^2$ and therefore does not require symmetry of $R$. Summation by parts gives $$|Q_k|^2-\sum_{l=0}^kw_l|Q_l|^2=\int x\,\,\mathrm d|Q|^2.$$ It remains to identify the endpoints. At $t=1$ the fields vanish and $\varphi(1)=F_{d,n}(D)+\lambda:D$. At $t=0$, remove the overlap constraint. This increases the terminal sum and hence every recursive value, because each integration is monotone. The resulting recursion factors over sites, giving $\varphi(0)\le\Psi(Q,x,\lambda)$. Integration of the derivative bound proves the proposition. Singular covariance increments are permitted by continuity, or directly by representing a field through a square root of its covariance. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The scalar functional

<a id="static-eq:scalar-upper-bound"></a>
The scalar version of the hierarchy gives the variational quantity against which we compare the unconstrained pressure. Let $\mu$ be a probability measure on $[0,1]$ and put $\alpha(s)=\mu([0,s])$. Define $\Phi_\alpha(s,z)$ by the terminal condition $\Phi_\alpha(1,z)=\log(2\cosh z)$ and the backward equation <a id="static-eq:scalar-pde"></a>$$\label{static-eq:scalar-pde}
 -\partial_s\Phi_\alpha
 =\frac{\beta^2}{2}
       \left(\partial_{zz}\Phi_\alpha
                    +\alpha(s)(\partial_z\Phi_\alpha)^2\right).$$ For a step function $\alpha$, the equation means the Gaussian recursion already defined: over an interval of length $b-a$ with constant mass $x$, its terminal function $f$ is mapped to $$z\longmapsto\frac1x\log\mathbb E
       \exp\{x f(z+\beta\sqrt{b-a}\,G)\},$$ with ordinary expectation at $x=0$. For general $\alpha$, take $L^1$ limits of step functions; Lemma <a href="#static-lem:scalar-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:scalar-regularity">9</a> proves that this definition exists, is independent of the approximation, and has the stability properties we use. Define <a id="static-eq:scalar-functional"></a>$$\label{static-eq:scalar-functional}
 \mathcal P_\beta(\mu)=\Phi_\alpha(0,0)
                   -\frac{\beta^2}{2}\int_0^1s\alpha(s)\,\mathrm ds,
 \qquad P_\beta=\inf_\mu\mathcal P_\beta(\mu).$$ The infimum is attained by compactness and continuity, as proved in Proposition <a href="#static-prop:scalar-minimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-prop:scalar-minimizer">10</a>. These scalar approximation and compactness arguments are independent of the pressure comparison; the lower comparison below will use only finitely supported trial measures. Apply Proposition <a href="#static-prop:matrix-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-prop:matrix-bound">5</a> with $d=1$, $D=Q_{\rm end}=1$, and $\lambda=0$. First for step measures and then by approximation, this gives the upper comparison $$\label{static-eq:scalar-upper-bound}
 p_n(\beta):=\frac1n\mathbb E\log Z_{n,\beta}\le P_\beta.$$

<a id="stmt-static-thm-finite-size"></a>
<div id="static-thm:finite-size" class="theorem">

**Theorem 6** (Quantitative comparison). *For every fixed $\beta>0$, there is $C_\beta<\infty$ such that for all sufficiently large $n$, <a id="static-eq:finite-size"></a>$$\label{static-eq:finite-size}
 P_\beta-C_\beta n^{-1/24}\le p_n(\beta)\le P_\beta.$$*

</div>

The remaining task is the lower comparison. We will choose an interpolation path from expectations of overlaps, using a hierarchical backward system. Such expectation-dependent choices are also a feature of the adaptive interpolation method of Barbier and Macris <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[BarbierMacris19](#ref-BarbierMacris19)]. Here we first prove an explicit overlap error estimate for the hierarchy, without assuming a prior description of the Gibbs measure, and then use it along the chosen path. Mourrat’s enriched-free-energy and Hamilton–Jacobi viewpoint <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Mourrat2020](#ref-Mourrat2020), Section 3, equations (3.24)–(3.27)] provides related motivation for organizing errors by hierarchical overlaps. This is a methodological connection only: the direct covariance and concentration argument and the quantitative bound here do not invoke a Hamilton–Jacobi theorem.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Concentration along a hierarchy

To obtain an error estimate that can be summed over the levels, we use equally spaced masses for the remainder of the section: $$w_j=\frac1{k+1},\qquad x_j=\frac{j+1}{k+1}\quad(0\le j\le k).$$ Fix $0\le t\le1$ and levels $0=q_{-1}<q_0<\cdots<q_k$. Set $\Delta_j=\beta^2(q_j-q_{j-1})$ and use the terminal exponent <a id="static-eq:adaptive-exponent"></a>$$\label{static-eq:adaptive-exponent}
 U(\sigma)=\sqrt t\,\beta\widehat H_n(\sigma)
              +\sum_{j=0}^k\sqrt{\Delta_j}\,z_j\cdot\sigma,$$ where $z_0,\ldots,z_k$ are independent standard Gaussian vectors in $\mathbb R^n$. Let $\mathcal A_j$ contain the prefix through level $j$, including the quenched Hamiltonian disorder. Under the path law, put <a id="static-eq:hierarchy-means"></a>$$\label{static-eq:hierarchy-means}
 m_j=\mathbb E[\sigma\mid\mathcal A_j],\qquad
 M_j=\frac{\|m_j\|^2}{n},\qquad r_j=\mathbb EM_j.$$ The conditional means $m_j$ form a martingale. Consequently $0\le r_0\le\cdots\le r_k\le1$. They can also be obtained by differentiating $F_j$ with respect to an additional deterministic field coupled linearly to the spins. We begin by controlling the Gaussian field energy under the same path law.

<a id="stmt-static-lem-field-energy"></a>
<div id="static-lem:field-energy" class="lemma">

<a id="static-eq:field-energy-concentration"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Field-energy concentration). *For every $0\le j\le k$, under the single-path law, $$\label{static-eq:field-energy-concentration}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}\left(\frac{z_j\cdot\sigma}{n}\right)\le\frac1n.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-static-lem-field-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-static-lem-field-energy</summary>

*Proof.* Condition first on the terminal spin $\sigma$. The product of the tilts in Equation <a href="#static-eq:hierarchy-tilt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:hierarchy-tilt">[static-eq:hierarchy-tilt]</a> and the terminal Gibbs density telescopes. Thus the conditional joint density of all standard Gaussian disorder variables and fields is proportional to their original Gaussian density multiplied by <a id="static-eq:fixed-spin-density"></a>$$\label{static-eq:fixed-spin-density}
 \exp\left\{U(\sigma)-\sum_{l=0}^kw_lF_l\right\}.$$ Each $F_l$ is jointly convex in its prefix Gaussian variables. At the terminal level this follows from log-sum-exp with linearly entering variables. Backwards integration preserves convexity: expectation does so at mass zero, and Hölder’s inequality does so for a positive-mass logarithmic expectation. Extend $F_l$ to all variables by making it constant in its descendants; it is still convex. The weights $w_l$ are nonnegative, and $U(\sigma)$ is linear for fixed $\sigma$. The negative logarithm of the conditional density therefore has Hessian at least $I$. The linear specialization of Lemma <a href="#static-lem:gaussian-tools" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:gaussian-tools">3</a> bounds the conditional variance of $z_j\cdot\sigma/n$ by $1/n$.

To remove the conditioning, we must also check the conditional mean. It is independent of $\sigma$, as follows from the gauge transformation with signs $\varepsilon_i=\sigma_i$: $$J_{ab}\longmapsto\varepsilon_a\varepsilon_bJ_{ab}\quad(a<b),
 \qquad z_{l,i}\longmapsto\varepsilon_i z_{l,i}\quad(0\le l\le k).$$ Leave the diagonal variables fixed. This transformation preserves the Gaussian law and carries the conditional law at $\sigma$ to the conditional law at the all-plus spin. It also preserves $z_j\cdot\sigma$. Thus the conditional means agree, and the law of total variance proves Equation <a href="#static-eq:field-energy-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:field-energy-concentration">[static-eq:field-energy-concentration]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-static-lem-average-overlap"></a>
<div id="static-lem:average-overlap" class="lemma">

<a id="static-eq:average-overlap-concentration"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Average overlap concentration). *Let $\Delta_{\min}=\min_j\Delta_j$. If $k\ge1$ and $k/\sqrt{n\Delta_{\min}}\le1$, then $$\label{static-eq:average-overlap-concentration}
 \sum_{j=0}^kw_j\mathbb E_j^{(2)}(R-r_j)^2
 \le C\left(\frac{k}{\sqrt{n\Delta_{\min}}}+k^{-1/2}\right),$$ where $C$ is an absolute constant.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-static-lem-average-overlap"><summary>Proof of stmt-static-lem-average-overlap</summary>

*Proof.* The law of total variance splits the overlap error into two terms: the fluctuation of its conditional mean $M_j$, and the variance left after the prefix is fixed. We estimate these terms in that order.

<a id="static-eq:tree-ibp"></a>
**Fluctuation of the conditional mean.** Fix $j<k$. Sample three paths $a,b,c$ with common prefix through $j+1$ and conditionally independent continuations. If $B=B(\sigma^a,\sigma^b,\sigma^c)$ satisfies $|B|\le1$, integration by parts in the two adjacent Gaussian fields gives $$\label{static-eq:tree-ibp}
 \mathbb EB\left(\frac{z_j}{\sqrt{\Delta_j}}
           -\frac{z_{j+1}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{j+1}}}\right)
                    \cdot\frac{\sigma^a}{n}
 =-w_j\mathbb EB\frac{m_j\cdot\sigma^a}{n}.$$ Here is the density calculation, including the common prefix. Relative to the product Gaussian law, multiply $W_l$ along each common edge $1\le l\le j+1$, along each of the three distinct descendant branches, and then by the three terminal Gibbs probabilities. The logarithm of this density is $$\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_{l=1}^{j+1}x_{l-1}(F_l-F_{l-1})\\
 &\quad+\sum_{v\in\{a,b,c\}}\left[
       \sum_{l=j+2}^kx_{l-1}(F_l^v-F_{l-1}^v)
                         +U^v(\sigma^v)-F_k^v\right].
\end{aligned}$$ Apply the derivative difference $\Delta_j^{-1/2}\partial_{z_{j,i}}
-\Delta_{j+1}^{-1/2}\partial_{z_{j+1,i}}$ coordinate by coordinate. At level $j+1$ and below, every term depends on these fields only through $\sqrt{\Delta_j}z_j+\sqrt{\Delta_{j+1}}z_{j+1}$, so its two derivatives cancel. Terms above $j$ depend on neither field. Only $F_j$ remains, with coefficient $x_{j-1}-x_j=-w_j$; the same calculation applies at $j=0$ because $x_{-1}=0$. Its normalized derivative is $m_{j,i}$. Throughout this differentiation the spin labels and $B$ are fixed. Integration by parts and summation over $i$ now prove Equation <a href="#static-eq:tree-ibp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:tree-ibp">[static-eq:tree-ibp]</a>.

<a id="static-eq:mean-overlap-variance"></a>
Let $Y$ be the quantity multiplying $B$ on the left-hand side of Equation <a href="#static-eq:tree-ibp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:tree-ibp">[static-eq:tree-ibp]</a>. Every branch has the single-path marginal. Lemma <a href="#static-lem:field-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:field-energy">7</a> and the $L^2$ triangle inequality therefore imply $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(Y)^{1/2}\le\frac{2}{\sqrt{n\Delta_{\min}}}.$$ With $B=1$, Equation <a href="#static-eq:tree-ibp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:tree-ibp">[static-eq:tree-ibp]</a> reads $\mathbb EY=-w_jr_j$. Subtract this mean, use $|B|\le1$, and recall $w_j=1/(k+1)$ to obtain <a id="static-eq:tree-centered"></a>$$\label{static-eq:tree-centered}
 \left|\mathbb EB\left(\frac{m_j\cdot\sigma^a}{n}-r_j\right)\right|
 \le\frac{2(k+1)}{\sqrt{n\Delta_{\min}}}.$$ Choose $B=R(\sigma^b,\sigma^c)$. Conditional on $\mathcal A_{j+1}$ the three descendants are independent, so the left-hand side becomes $$\left|\mathbb E\left(\frac{m_j\cdot m_{j+1}}n-r_j\right)M_{j+1}\right|.$$ We now replace $m_{j+1}$ by $m_j$ in both factors. The change in the expectation is at most $3\sqrt{\gamma_j}$, where $$\gamma_j=\frac1n\mathbb E\|m_{j+1}-m_j\|^2.$$ This follows from the bounds $\|m_l\|\le\sqrt n$ and $|M_j-r_j|\le1$. After replacement the expectation is $\mathbb E(M_j-r_j)M_j=\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(M_j)$. Martingale orthogonality gives $\sum_{j<k}\gamma_j\le1$; averaging the errors and using Cauchy–Schwarz proves $$\label{static-eq:mean-overlap-variance}
 \sum_{j<k}w_j\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(M_j)
 \le C\left(\frac{k}{\sqrt{n\Delta_{\min}}}+k^{-1/2}\right).$$

<a id="static-eq:hessian-covariance-bound"></a>
**Conditional variance.** The conditional mean fluctuation is now controlled. To bound the variance of the overlap of two descendants after their common prefix is fixed, define $$C_j=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(\sigma\mid\mathcal A_j),\qquad
 D_j=\nabla^2F_j,\qquad
 V_j=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(m_{j+1}\mid\mathcal A_j),$$ where all conditional quantities are given $\mathcal A_j$, and $D_j$ is the Hessian in an additive deterministic spin field. The law of total covariance and the differentiated recursion give $$C_j=\mathbb E[C_{j+1}\mid\mathcal A_j]+V_j,
 \qquad
 D_j=\mathbb E[D_{j+1}\mid\mathcal A_j]+x_jV_j.$$ The terminal identity $D_k=C_k$, together with the nondecreasing masses, allows backward induction and gives $$\label{static-eq:hessian-covariance-bound}
 D_j\succeq x_jC_j.$$

<a id="static-eq:covariance-schur-bound"></a>
For the next field, abbreviate $\Delta=\Delta_{j+1}$ and set $y=\sqrt\Delta z_{j+1}$. Conditional on $\mathcal A_j$, its density is proportional to $\exp\{-\|y\|^2/(2\Delta)+x_jF_{j+1}\}$. Integration by parts, using $\nabla_yF_{j+1}=m_{j+1}$, yields <a id="static-eq:conditional-cross-covariance"></a><a id="static-eq:conditional-field-covariance"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(m_{j+1},y\mid\mathcal A_j)&=\Delta D_j,
 \label{static-eq:conditional-cross-covariance}\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(y\mid\mathcal A_j)&=\Delta I+\Delta^2x_jD_j.
 \label{static-eq:conditional-field-covariance}
\end{aligned}$$ For the first identity, use $\mathbb E[y\mid\mathcal A_j]=\Delta x_jm_j$ and subtract the product of means from the integration-by-parts expression for $\mathbb E[m_{j+1}y^{\mathsf T}\mid\mathcal A_j]$. A further application to $yy^{\mathsf T}$ proves the second identity. The joint covariance matrix of $(m_{j+1},y)$ is positive semidefinite, so its Schur complement gives $$\label{static-eq:covariance-schur-bound}
 V_j\succeq\Delta D_j(I+\Delta x_jD_j)^{-1}D_j.$$

<a id="static-eq:average-square-trace"></a>
Order the eigenvalues of $C_j,D_j,V_j$ decreasingly and denote them by $c_i,d_i,v_i$, respectively. Equation <a href="#static-eq:hessian-covariance-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:hessian-covariance-bound">[static-eq:hessian-covariance-bound]</a> implies $d_i\ge x_jc_i$. The function $d\mapsto\Delta d^2/(1+\Delta x_jd)$ is increasing on $d\ge0$. Apply the min–max principle to Equation <a href="#static-eq:covariance-schur-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:covariance-schur-bound">[static-eq:covariance-schur-bound]</a> to get $$v_i\ge\frac{\Delta d_i^2}{1+\Delta x_jd_i}
      \ge\frac{\Delta x_j^2c_i^2}{1+\Delta x_j^2c_i}.$$ At or above the threshold $c_i\ge(\Delta x_j^2)^{-1}$, the final expression is at least $c_i/2$. Since also $c_i\le\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}C_j\le n$, these eigenvalues contribute at most $2n\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}V_j$ to $\sum_i c_i^2$. Those below the threshold contribute at most $n/(\Delta x_j^2)$. Together the two bounds give <a id="static-eq:conditional-square-trace"></a>$$\label{static-eq:conditional-square-trace}
 \frac{\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}C_j^2}{n^2}
 \le\frac{2\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}V_j}{n}
             +\frac1{n\Delta_{j+1}x_j^2}.$$ Martingale orthogonality again supplies the summable quantity: $\sum_{j<k}\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}V_j\le n$. Sum over $j$, using $x_j=(j+1)/(k+1)$, to obtain $$\label{static-eq:average-square-trace}
 B_0:=\sum_{j<k}w_j\mathbb E\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}C_j^2}{n^2}
 \le\frac Ck+\frac{Ck}{n\Delta_{\min}}.$$

For two independent descendants of the level-$j$ prefix, the conditional overlap has mean $M_j$ and variance $$\frac1{n^2}\left(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}C_j^2+2m_j^{\mathsf T}C_jm_j\right).$$ The contribution of the second term is at most $2\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}C_j^2/n^2}$: use $\|m_j\|^2\le n$ and $\|C_j\|_{\rm op}\le\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}C_j^2}$. Thus the averaged conditional variance is at most $B_0+2\sqrt{B_0}$. Under $k/\sqrt{n\Delta_{\min}}\le1$, Equation <a href="#static-eq:average-square-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:average-square-trace">[static-eq:average-square-trace]</a> gives $B_0\le C/k$. Combine this estimate with the conditional-mean bound <a href="#static-eq:mean-overlap-variance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:mean-overlap-variance">[static-eq:mean-overlap-variance]</a> through the law of total variance. Finally, the level $k$ costs at most $4/(k+1)$ because $|R-r_k|\le2$. These bounds prove Equation <a href="#static-eq:average-overlap-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:average-overlap-concentration">[static-eq:average-overlap-concentration]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Adaptive interpolation and the finite-size error

<details id="proof-stmt-static-lem-average-overlap-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-static-lem-average-overlap</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#static-thm:finite-size" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-thm:finite-size">6</a>.* The upper comparison was proved in Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-upper-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-upper-bound">[static-eq:scalar-upper-bound]</a>. To obtain the lower comparison, we choose the field levels so that the pressure derivative separates into a quadratic expression in the overlap means and the overlap error controlled by Lemma <a href="#static-lem:average-overlap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:average-overlap">8</a>. After constructing that path, we will integrate the derivative and identify its single-site endpoint with a scalar trial measure.

<a id="static-eq:field-gap-derivative"></a>
<a id="static-eq:adaptive-ode"></a>
<a id="static-eq:adaptive-pressure-derivative"></a>
**Choosing the adaptive path.** Consider provisionally a differentiable path $q(t)$ with positive gaps, and set $f(t)=n^{-1}\mathbb EF_0(t,q(t))$. For a field gap in overlap units, $a_j=q_j-q_{j-1}$, the hierarchy identity gives $$\label{static-eq:field-gap-derivative}
 \frac{\partial}{\partial a_j}\frac1n\mathbb EF_0
 =\frac{\beta^2}{2}
       \left(1-\sum_{l=j}^kw_lr_l\right).$$ The Hamiltonian contribution to the derivative is $\frac{\beta^2}{4}(1-\sum_lw_l\mathbb E_l^{(2)}R^2)$. Summing the field contributions and using $\sum_{j=0}^la_j'=q_l'$ therefore gives $$f'(t)=\frac{\beta^2}{4}
       \left(1-\sum_{l=0}^kw_l\mathbb E_l^{(2)}R^2\right)
       +\frac{\beta^2}{2}
       \left(q_k'-\sum_{l=0}^kw_lr_lq_l'\right).$$ This calculation suggests the system $$\label{static-eq:adaptive-ode}
 q_j'(t)=-r_j(t,q(t)).$$ Indeed, since $\mathbb E_j^{(2)}R=r_j$, every positive-gap solution satisfies $$\label{static-eq:adaptive-pressure-derivative}
 f'(t)=\frac{\beta^2}{4}\left[
 1-2r_k+\sum_{j=0}^kw_jr_j^2
          -\sum_{j=0}^kw_j\mathbb E_j^{(2)}(R-r_j)^2\right].$$ The final term is exactly the error already estimated; the remaining terms can be bounded after integration using the differential equation. We now construct a solution with gaps large enough to apply that estimate. Choose $$k=\lfloor n^{1/12}\rfloor,\qquad \eta=n^{-1/6},
 \qquad q_j(1)=(j+1)\eta\quad(0\le j\le k),$$ and solve Equation <a href="#static-eq:adaptive-ode" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:adaptive-ode">[static-eq:adaptive-ode]</a> backwards from $t=1$.

**Existence and bounds for the adaptive path.** We verify that this system has a solution throughout $[0,1]$ and keeps every gap positive. On the open region of positive gaps, the finite Gaussian recursions make $r_j$ continuous in $t$ and locally Lipschitz in $q$. The following domination justifies those assertions. Fix a compact subset of this region; in this local argument constants may depend on $n,k,\beta$ and that subset. Uniformly in the prefix and in $t\in[0,1]$, we have $\|\nabla_{z_h}F_h\|=\sqrt{\Delta_h}\|m_h\|\le L$. Subtract the value of $F_h$ at $z_h=0$ from both numerator and denominator in the normalized tilt. This gives $$W_h(z_h)\le C\exp(L\|z_h\|).$$ The conditional first moments of subsequent fields are consequently uniformly bounded. The derivative $\partial_{q_i}U$ is bounded by a constant times $\|z_i\|+\|z_{i+1}\|$, with the second term omitted at $i=k$. Differentiation through the recursions therefore yields $$|\partial_{q_i}F_l|\le C\left(1+\sum_{h\le l}\|z_h\|\right).$$ To apply this to $r_j$, represent it as the expectation of $R$ on a two-path tree. Since terminal Gibbs probabilities are at most one, the preceding estimates dominate its density and the $q_i$ derivative of that density by $C(1+S)e^{LS}$, where $S$ is the sum of the norms of all fields in this finite tree. The bound is Gaussian integrable, uniformly in $t$ and in the chosen compact set of $q$ values. It justifies the differentiated recursions and gives the required local Lipschitz property. At $t=0$, continuity follows from the same bound and continuity in $\sqrt t$; no derivative of $\sqrt t$ is needed.

<a id="static-eq:adaptive-overlap-error"></a>
Along the local solution, $r_j\ge r_{j-1}$ implies that each gap $q_j-q_{j-1}$ is nondecreasing as time runs backwards, with $r_{-1}=0$. Each gap therefore stays at least $\eta$. The bound $0\le r_j\le1$ also prevents escape to infinity and gives <a id="static-eq:adaptive-path-bounds"></a>$$\label{static-eq:adaptive-path-bounds}
 q_k(t)\le(k+1)\eta+1-t,
 \qquad\Delta_j(t)\ge\beta^2\eta.$$ These estimates continue the local solution across the full interval. They also verify the field-gap hypothesis of Lemma <a href="#static-lem:average-overlap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:average-overlap">8</a>. Thus, for all sufficiently large $n$, uniformly along the path, $$\label{static-eq:adaptive-overlap-error}
 \sum_{j=0}^kw_j\mathbb E_j^{(2)}(R-r_j)^2
 \le C_\beta n^{-1/24}.$$ Indeed, the two terms in that lemma are controlled by $k/\sqrt{n\beta^2\eta}=O_\beta(n^{-1/3})$ and $k^{-1/2}=O(n^{-1/24})$.

<a id="static-eq:adaptive-lower-bound"></a>
**Integration of the pressure derivative.** The adaptive path has now been constructed and its overlap error is bounded, so we can integrate Equation <a href="#static-eq:adaptive-pressure-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:adaptive-pressure-derivative">[static-eq:adaptive-pressure-derivative]</a>. Write $\delta=(k+1)\eta=O(n^{-1/12})$ and $Q_j=q_j(0)$. The derivative in Equation <a href="#static-eq:field-gap-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:field-gap-derivative">[static-eq:field-gap-derivative]</a> lies between $0$ and $\beta^2/2$. Consequently, removing the fields present at $t=1$ costs at most $\beta^2\delta/2$, and $$f(1)\le p_n(\beta)+\frac{\beta^2}{2}\delta.$$ The differential equation and Jensen’s inequality also give $$\int_0^1r_j(t)\,\mathrm dt=Q_j-q_j(1),\qquad
 \int_0^1r_j(t)^2\,\mathrm dt\ge\bigl(Q_j-q_j(1)\bigr)^2.$$ The endpoint bounds are $0\le Q_j\le1+\delta$ and $0\le q_j(1)\le\delta$. Integrate Equation <a href="#static-eq:adaptive-pressure-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:adaptive-pressure-derivative">[static-eq:adaptive-pressure-derivative]</a>, apply these bounds and the overlap error in Equation <a href="#static-eq:adaptive-overlap-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:adaptive-overlap-error">[static-eq:adaptive-overlap-error]</a>, and conclude that $$\label{static-eq:adaptive-lower-bound}
 p_n(\beta)\ge
 f(0)+\frac{\beta^2}{4}
       \left(1-2Q_k+\sum_{j=0}^kw_jQ_j^2\right)
       -C_\beta n^{-1/24}.$$

**Identification of a scalar trial measure.** At $t=0$ there is no Hamiltonian coupling, so the recursion factors over sites. Thus $f(0)$ is the scalar single-site value at levels $Q_0,\ldots,Q_k$. The only possible obstruction to using these levels as the support of a trial measure on $[0,1]$ is that some may exceed one.

Replace each such level by $\overline Q_j=\min(Q_j,1)$. Each gap can only decrease, and their total decrease is $Q_k-\overline Q_k\le\delta$. Equation <a href="#static-eq:field-gap-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:field-gap-derivative">[static-eq:field-gap-derivative]</a> bounds the change in the scalar value by $\beta^2\delta/2$. The quadratic expression in Equation <a href="#static-eq:adaptive-lower-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:adaptive-lower-bound">[static-eq:adaptive-lower-bound]</a> changes by at most $C_\beta\delta$ as well. We may therefore take $Q_k\le1$ within the stated error, interpreting zero gaps by continuity.

Now define $\mu=\sum_{j=0}^kw_j\delta_{Q_j}$. Its distribution function is zero up to $Q_0$, equals $x_{j-1}$ on each gap $(Q_{j-1},Q_j)$, and equals one between $Q_k$ and $1$. Over this final interval, the recursion maps $\log(2\cosh z)$ to $\log(2\cosh z)+\beta^2(1-Q_k)/2$. Hence $$\Phi_\alpha(0,0)=f(0)+\frac{\beta^2}{2}(1-Q_k).$$ The penalty is identified by summation by parts: $$\frac{\beta^2}{2}\int_0^1s\alpha(s)\,\mathrm ds
 =\frac{\beta^2}{4}\left(1-\sum_{j=0}^kw_jQ_j^2\right).$$ The expression preceding the error in Equation <a href="#static-eq:adaptive-lower-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:adaptive-lower-bound">[static-eq:adaptive-lower-bound]</a> is therefore exactly $\mathcal P_\beta(\mu)$. It is at least $P_\beta$, which completes the lower comparison and the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="static-sec:scalar"></a>
### Scalar regularity and properties of a minimizer

We now establish the scalar identities used in the overlap comparisons and the equilibrium energy estimate. The argument has three parts. First, we justify the scalar equation and its stochastic evolution for general distribution functions, including those with atoms. Next, we derive first variations and use them to locate the support of an arbitrary minimizing measure. Finally, the resulting stationarity identity gives a strict energy bound when $\beta>1$. No further description of the minimizer’s support is assumed.

Regularity and step approximation for the Parisi evolution, together with zero-field support properties of its minimizers, were developed by Auffinger and Chen <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[AC2015](#ref-AC2015), Propositions 1–2 and Theorems 1, 5]. The full first-variation characterization appears in Chen <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Chen2017](#ref-Chen2017), Theorem 2 and Proposition 1] and, independently, in Jagannath and Tobasco <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[JT2017](#ref-JT2017), Proposition 1.1, Corollary 3.10 and Remark 1.3]. We prove the required statements here with the normalization and atom conventions used by the later pressure comparisons.

<a id="static-eq:scalar-pde-recalled"></a>
Throughout this section, $\alpha(s)=\mu([0,s])$ is the right-continuous distribution function of a probability measure on $[0,1]$. Recall the scalar terminal-value equation $$\label{static-eq:scalar-pde-recalled}
 -\partial_s\Phi_\alpha
 =\frac{\beta^2}{2}\bigl(\partial_{zz}\Phi_\alpha
          +\alpha(s)(\partial_z\Phi_\alpha)^2\bigr),
 \qquad \Phi_\alpha(1,z)=\log(2\cosh z).$$ For a step distribution function this is the Gaussian recursion from the preceding section. For general distribution functions, the scalar values are defined by approximation, which the next lemma justifies. The time derivative in the equation is understood almost everywhere; no differentiability at a jump of $\alpha$ is required. We denote the first two spatial derivatives by $u=\partial_z\Phi_\alpha$ and $\chi=\partial_{zz}\Phi_\alpha$.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Regularity and first variations

<a id="stmt-static-lem-scalar-regularity"></a>
<div id="static-lem:scalar-regularity" class="lemma">

<a id="static-eq:scalar-stability"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Regularity, stability, and scalar evolution). *The step approximation defining $\Phi_\alpha$ is independent of the approximating sequence. The functions $\Phi_\alpha,u,\chi$ are continuous on $[0,1]\times\mathbb R$, with <a id="static-eq:scalar-derivative-bounds"></a>$$\label{static-eq:scalar-derivative-bounds}
 |u|\le1,\qquad 0<\chi\le1,\qquad
 |\chi(s,z)-\chi(s,z')|\le C_\beta|z-z'|.$$ The function $\Phi_\alpha(s,\cdot)$ is even, so $u(s,0)=0$. For two distribution functions $\alpha,\widetilde\alpha$, $$\label{static-eq:scalar-stability}
 \sup_{s,z}\bigl(
 |\Phi_\alpha-\Phi_{\widetilde\alpha}|
 +|u_\alpha-u_{\widetilde\alpha}|
 +|\chi_\alpha-\chi_{\widetilde\alpha}|
 \bigr)
 \le C_\beta\int_0^1|\alpha(s)-\widetilde\alpha(s)|\,\mathrm ds.$$ These functions are also continuous in $\beta>0$, uniformly in $(s,z)$ for their differences, locally in $\beta$.*

<a id="static-eq:scalar-diffusion"></a>
<a id="static-eq:scalar-martingale"></a>
<a id="static-eq:scalar-chi-expectation"></a>
<a id="eq:scalar-evolution"></a>
<a id="static-eq:gamma-derivative"></a>
*The stochastic differential equation $$\label{static-eq:scalar-diffusion}
 \,\mathrm dX_s=\beta\,\mathrm dB_s+\beta^2\alpha(s)u(s,X_s)\,\mathrm ds,
 \qquad X_0=0,$$ has a unique strong solution. For a step distribution function, its transitions across the constant-mass intervals are the normalized tilted Gaussian kernels of the scalar recursion. Its bounded martingale $U_s=u(s,X_s)$ satisfies $$\label{static-eq:scalar-martingale}
 U_t-U_s=\beta\int_s^t\chi(v,X_v)\,\mathrm dB_v.$$ Moreover, $$\label{static-eq:scalar-chi-expectation}
 \mathbb E\chi(t,X_t)-\mathbb E\chi(s,X_s)
 =-\beta^2\int_s^t\alpha(v)\mathbb E\chi(v,X_v)^2\,\mathrm dv.$$ For $0\le s<t\le1$, the conditional form is $$\label{eq:scalar-evolution}
 \mathbb E[\chi(t,X_t)\mid X_s]
 =\chi(s,X_s)-\beta^2\int_s^t\alpha(v)
                   \mathbb E[\chi(v,X_v)^2\mid X_s]\,\mathrm dv.$$ Consequently $\Gamma(s):=\mathbb Eu(s,X_s)^2$ is continuously differentiable, with one-sided derivatives at the endpoints, and $$\label{static-eq:gamma-derivative}
 \Gamma(0)=0,\qquad
 \Gamma'(s)=\beta^2\mathbb E\chi(s,X_s)^2.$$ The well-posedness statement and the evolution identities <a href="#static-eq:scalar-martingale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-martingale">[static-eq:scalar-martingale]</a>–<a href="#static-eq:scalar-chi-expectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-chi-expectation">[static-eq:scalar-chi-expectation]</a> also hold for a diffusion started at any deterministic time and spatial value.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-static-lem-scalar-regularity"><summary>Proof of stmt-static-lem-scalar-regularity</summary>

*Proof.* We prove estimates uniform over step distribution functions, use them to pass to general $\alpha$, and then establish continuity in the temperature parameter.

**Derivative bounds along a finite step path.** Suppose first that $\alpha$ is a step function. On an interval of constant mass $m$, its recursion is $$T_m f(z)=m^{-1}\log\mathbb Ee^{m f(z+\beta\sqrt{\ell}\,Z)}$$ where $\ell$ is the interval length and $T_0f=\mathbb Ef(z+\beta\sqrt{\ell}\,Z)$. To keep track of the successive tilts, fix $(s,z)$ and list the remaining breakpoints as $s=t_0<\cdots<t_r=1$. Let $m_j$ be the mass on $[t_j,t_{j+1})$ and set $f_j=\Phi_\alpha(t_j,\cdot)$. Starting with $Z_0=z$, sample $Z_{j+1}$ from $$K_j(v,\,\mathrm dy)
 =\exp\{m_j[f_{j+1}(y)-f_j(v)]\}
       N(v,\beta^2(t_{j+1}-t_j))(\,\mathrm dy),$$ where the exponential equals one at $m_j=0$. Each kernel is normalized by the scalar recursion on its own interval. Put $\mathcal F_j=\sigma(Z_0,\ldots,Z_j)$ and $M_j=f_j'(Z_j)$. Differentiating the recursion once and twice gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[M_{j+1}\mid\mathcal F_j]&=M_j,\\
 f_j''(Z_j)&=\mathbb E[f_{j+1}''(Z_{j+1})\mid\mathcal F_j]
      +m_j\mathbb E[(M_{j+1}-M_j)^2\mid\mathcal F_j].
\end{aligned}$$ Thus $M$ is a martingale for the concatenated kernels, even though the tilts on successive intervals can differ. Its terminal value is $M_r=\tanh Z_r$. Iterating the second identity yields $$\chi(s,z)=\mathbb E\operatorname{sech}^2(Z_r)
           +\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}m_j\mathbb E(M_{j+1}-M_j)^2.$$ The increments are orthogonal, so their unweighted squared sum has expectation $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\tanh Z_r)$. Since $0\le m_j\le1$, we obtain $$0<\mathbb E\bigl[1-\tanh^2(Z_r)\bigr]
 \le\chi(s,z)
 \le \mathbb E\bigl[1-\tanh^2(Z_r)\bigr]
       +\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\tanh Z_r)
 =1-u(s,z)^2\le1.$$ Every recursion preserves evenness. Its gradient is a conditional mean of the terminal gradient, which gives $|u|\le1$.

Before differentiating the equation, we record why all required spatial derivatives exist and are bounded for a fixed finite path. The $k$th derivative of one logarithmic Gaussian integral is a polynomial in tilted moments of $f',\ldots,f^{(k)}$, with coefficients bounded for $0\le m\le1$. At $m=0$, differentiation passes directly through Gaussian expectation. All positive-order derivatives of $\log(2\cosh z)$ are bounded, so finite induction gives bounded derivatives at the breakpoints and within each interval. The scalar values have at most linear growth, ensuring Gaussian integrability at every step. These preliminary bounds may depend on the path; we next obtain the uniform bound needed for approximation.

Within a constant-mass interval the recursion is smooth and solves Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-pde-recalled" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-pde-recalled">[static-eq:scalar-pde-recalled]</a>. Its first two spatial derivatives satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 -u_s&=\frac{\beta^2}{2}u_{zz}+\beta^2\alpha u u_z,\\
 -\chi_s&=\frac{\beta^2}{2}\chi_{zz}
       +\beta^2\alpha u\chi_z+\beta^2\alpha\chi^2.
\end{aligned}$$ Set $w=\chi_z$. One more differentiation gives a linear equation: $$-w_s=\frac{\beta^2}{2}w_{zz}
       +\beta^2\alpha u w_z+3\beta^2\alpha\chi w.$$ The bounds already proved for $u$ and $\chi$ control this equation’s drift and potential uniformly over all step paths. Its terminal value has absolute value at most $2$. Concatenate the diffusion representations over the finitely many intervals to obtain $$\sup_{s,z}|w(s,z)|\le2e^{3\beta^2}.$$ These differentiations may all be performed first inside the finite Gaussian recursions. Their bounded derivatives justify passage under the integrals, so the uniform estimate does not presuppose any regularity of the limiting path.

**The scalar diffusion and its evolution identities.** We next identify the tilted kernels with the diffusion in Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-diffusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-diffusion">[static-eq:scalar-diffusion]</a>. On a constant-mass interval $[a,b]$, start Brownian motion at $z$ by setting $Y_s=z+\beta(B_s-B_a)$. Under this law, Itô’s formula identifies $$\exp\{m(\Phi(b,Y_b)-\Phi(a,z))\}$$ as the exponential martingale with stochastic logarithm $m\beta\int_a^b u(v,Y_v)\,\mathrm dB_v$. Its bounded integrand justifies the change of measure, which adds drift $\beta^2m u$. Under that drift, a further application of Itô’s formula gives $$\,\mathrm du(s,X_s)=\beta\chi(s,X_s)\,\mathrm dB_s,
 \qquad
 \,\mathrm d\chi(s,X_s)=\beta\chi_z(s,X_s)\,\mathrm dB_s
                 -\beta^2\alpha(s)\chi(s,X_s)^2\,\mathrm ds.$$ This establishes both evolution identities for step paths.

**Stability and passage to general distribution functions.** The remaining task is to make these constructions independent of the step approximation. Subtract the equations for two step paths, keeping the same diffusion coefficient. The difference of the scalar values has drift $\frac{\beta^2}{2}\alpha(u+\widetilde u)$ and source $\frac{\beta^2}{2}(\alpha-\widetilde\alpha)\widetilde u^2$. The equation for $u-\widetilde u$ has bounded drift and potential, with source bounded by $C_\beta|\alpha-\widetilde\alpha|$. For $\chi-\widetilde\chi$, the drift and potential remain bounded, and the source is bounded by $$C_\beta\bigl(|\alpha-\widetilde\alpha|(s)
                 +\sup_z|u(s,z)-\widetilde u(s,z)|\bigr).$$ To obtain the last bound, expand $\alpha u\chi_z-\widetilde\alpha\widetilde u\widetilde\chi_z$ and use the uniform bound on $\widetilde\chi_z$. In the remaining reaction term, factor the difference of squares. Each difference has zero terminal value, so the diffusion representation of these linear equations proves Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-stability">[static-eq:scalar-stability]</a>, first for $\Phi$, then for $u$, and finally for $\chi$.

Approximate an arbitrary distribution function in $L^1$ by step distribution functions. The stability estimates make $\Phi,u,\chi$ converge uniformly, with uniform convergence of differences for the unbounded scalar value $\Phi$. The fundamental theorem of calculus in $z$ identifies the limits of $u$ and $\chi$ with the first two spatial derivatives of the limiting $\Phi$. Continuity, symmetry, and all non-strict bounds follow by passage to the limit. Passing to the limit in the time-integrated step equation also gives Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-pde-recalled" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-pde-recalled">[static-eq:scalar-pde-recalled]</a> almost everywhere in time.

The limiting drift in Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-diffusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-diffusion">[static-eq:scalar-diffusion]</a> is measurable in time, bounded, and uniformly Lipschitz in space. Hence the equation has a strong solution and pathwise uniqueness. Couple the solutions for two step approximations with the same Brownian motion; Gronwall’s inequality gives convergence uniformly in time. We may therefore also pass the terminal lower bound for $\chi$ to the limit: $$\chi(s,z)\ge\mathbb E_{s,z}\operatorname{sech}^2(X_1)>0.$$ Since $X_1$ is finite almost surely, this lower bound is strictly positive. Itô isometry passes the martingale identity to the limit, and bounded convergence does the same for the expectation identity. Nothing in these arguments requires the starting point to be $(0,0)$; they apply at every deterministic starting time and spatial value. Applying the expectation identity to a diffusion started at $(s,z)$ and then using the Markov property gives Equation <a href="#eq:scalar-evolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-evolution">[eq:scalar-evolution]</a>. Finally, Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-martingale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-martingale">[static-eq:scalar-martingale]</a> and the continuity of $\chi$ and $X$ give Equation <a href="#static-eq:gamma-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:gamma-derivative">[static-eq:gamma-derivative]</a>.

**Continuity in the temperature parameter.** Write $b=\beta^2$ and repeat the subtraction argument with different values of $b$. In the equation for $\Phi$, the additional source is $(\widetilde b-b)(\widetilde\chi+
\alpha\widetilde u^2)/2$, bounded by $C|\widetilde b-b|$. The bound on $\widetilde\chi_z$ gives the same source bound for $u$. Thus the differences of $\Phi$ and $u$ are $O(|\widetilde b-b|)$ locally uniformly for $b>0$. To obtain continuity of $\chi$, use its uniform spatial Lipschitz bound and approximate it by a difference quotient of $u$. For any $h>0$, $$\sup_{s,z}|\chi-\widetilde\chi|
 \le\frac{2}{h}\sup_{s,z}|u-\widetilde u|+C h.$$ Choose $h$ small first, then take $\widetilde b$ sufficiently close to $b$. This proves the required uniform continuity of $\chi$. The step approximation transfers these parameter estimates to all distribution functions. ◻

</details>

<a id="static-eq:scalar-mass-variation"></a>
<a id="static-eq:scalar-temperature-variation"></a>
We next differentiate the scalar functional in two directions: a change of its distribution function and a change of temperature with the distribution function fixed. We prove both formulas by differences, so they require no differentiability of a minimizing measure. For another distribution function $\widetilde\alpha$, set $\alpha_\theta=\alpha+\theta(\widetilde\alpha-\alpha)$. The first variation is $$\label{static-eq:scalar-mass-variation}
 \left.\frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm d\theta}\mathcal P_\beta(\alpha_\theta)
       \right|_{\theta=0+}
 =\frac{\beta^2}{2}\int_0^1
       (\widetilde\alpha-\alpha)(s)(\Gamma(s)-s)\,\mathrm ds.$$ We use $\mathcal P_\beta(\alpha)$ to denote the functional of the measure having distribution function $\alpha$. With $\alpha$ held fixed, the temperature derivative of the scalar value is $$\label{static-eq:scalar-temperature-variation}
 \frac{\partial}{\partial(\beta^2)}\Phi_\alpha(0,0)
 =\frac12\int_0^1
     \mathbb E\bigl[\chi(s,X_s)+\alpha(s)u(s,X_s)^2\bigr]\,\mathrm ds.$$ For each formula, start with step paths, where the equations are classical between finitely many breakpoints. Subtraction gives an integral representation, and that representation passes to general paths by the stability lemma.

<a id="static-eq:scalar-mass-difference"></a>
**Variation of the distribution function.** Subtract the equations for $\Phi_{\alpha_\theta}$ and $\Phi_\alpha$. The resulting drift is $\beta^2\alpha(u_{\alpha_\theta}+u_\alpha)/2$, and the source is $\theta\beta^2(\widetilde\alpha-\alpha)u_{\alpha_\theta}^2/2$. Introduce $Y^\theta$ by $Y^\theta_0=0$ and $$\,\mathrm dY_s^\theta=\beta\,\mathrm dB_s
 +\frac{\beta^2}{2}\alpha(s)
       (u_{\alpha_\theta}+u_\alpha)(s,Y_s^\theta)\,\mathrm ds.$$ The diffusion representation of the difference equation is the exact identity $$\label{static-eq:scalar-mass-difference}
 \frac{\Phi_{\alpha_\theta}(0,0)-\Phi_\alpha(0,0)}{\theta}
 =\frac{\beta^2}{2}\mathbb E\int_0^1
       (\widetilde\alpha-\alpha)(s)
       u_{\alpha_\theta}(s,Y_s^\theta)^2\,\mathrm ds.$$ For general $\alpha,\widetilde\alpha$, choose step approximations converging to each in $L^1$, and take their convex combinations at a fixed $\theta>0$. Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-stability">[static-eq:scalar-stability]</a> shows that the mixed drifts converge in integrated spatial supremum norm. They also have a common spatial Lipschitz bound. A coupling with the same Brownian motion and Gronwall’s inequality therefore gives uniform convergence of the corresponding $Y^\theta$ processes. The sources converge in integrated expectation by $|u|\le1$, the spatial Lipschitz bound for $u$, its uniform convergence, and the $L^1$ convergence of $\widetilde\alpha-\alpha$. Hence Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-mass-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-mass-difference">[static-eq:scalar-mass-difference]</a> holds for arbitrary distribution functions.

We may now send $\theta\downarrow0$. The same estimates give $Y^\theta\to X$ and uniform convergence of $u_{\alpha_\theta}$ to $u_\alpha$. The right-hand side converges to $\frac{\beta^2}{2}\int(\widetilde\alpha-\alpha)\Gamma$. Subtract the derivative of the linear penalty to obtain Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-mass-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-mass-variation">[static-eq:scalar-mass-variation]</a>.

<a id="static-eq:scalar-temperature-difference"></a>
**Variation of the temperature.** Set $b=\beta^2$ and use a superscript $b$ for scalar functions with parameter $\sqrt b$. For $\widetilde b\ne b$, let $Y^{b,\widetilde b}_0=0$ and define its evolution by $$\,\mathrm dY_s^{b,\widetilde b}=\sqrt b\,\mathrm dB_s
 +\frac b2\alpha(s)(u^b+u^{\widetilde b})
                (s,Y_s^{b,\widetilde b})\,\mathrm ds.$$ Subtract the two step-path equations with diffusion coefficient $b/2$ to obtain $$\label{static-eq:scalar-temperature-difference}
 \frac{\Phi^{\widetilde b}(0,0)-\Phi^b(0,0)}{\widetilde b-b}
 =\frac12\mathbb E\int_0^1
       [\chi^{\widetilde b}+\alpha(u^{\widetilde b})^2]
                    (s,Y_s^{b,\widetilde b})\,\mathrm ds.$$ For fixed $b,\widetilde b$, pass to general $\alpha$ by the same step approximation and Brownian coupling used above. The additional term involving $\chi$ is controlled by its uniform spatial Lipschitz bound. Next let $\widetilde b\to b$. Parameter continuity in Lemma <a href="#static-lem:scalar-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:scalar-regularity">9</a> and the coupling give $Y^{b,\widetilde b}\to X$. The integrands remain bounded and converge in integrated expectation. The limit in Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-temperature-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-temperature-difference">[static-eq:scalar-temperature-difference]</a> therefore proves Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-temperature-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-temperature-variation">[static-eq:scalar-temperature-variation]</a> for approach from either side.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Minimizers and the energy gap

The mass variation converts minimization into a condition on the support. We will use that condition both to identify the expected scalar susceptibility at every time and to show that the minimizing measure is nontrivial when $\beta>1$.

<a id="stmt-static-prop-scalar-minimizer"></a>
<div id="static-prop:scalar-minimizer" class="proposition">

**Proposition 10** (Support and stationarity identities). *The scalar functional has a minimizer $\mu$. For every such minimizer, with its associated scalar process, the following assertions hold:*

1.  *$0\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu$, and $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu\subset[0,1-\varepsilon_0]$ for some $\varepsilon_0>0$.*

2.  *At every $q\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu\cap(0,1)$, $\Gamma(q)=q$ and $\Gamma'(q)\le1$. At $q=0$, $\Gamma(0)=0$ and $\Gamma'(0)\le1$.*

<a id="static-eq:scalar-A-d-definitions"></a>
<a id="static-eq:scalar-stationarity-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:scalar-stationarity"></a>
<a id="static-eq:scalar-susceptibility"></a>
3.  *Define $$\label{static-eq:scalar-A-d-definitions}
       d_*(s)=1-\int_{(s,1]}t\,\mu(\,\mathrm dt),\qquad
       A(s)=\int_s^1\alpha(t)\,\mathrm dt=d_*(s)-s\alpha(s).$$ Then, at every $s\in[0,1]$, $$\label{static-eq:scalar-stationarity-identity}
       \mathbb E\bigl[\chi(s,X_s)+\alpha(s)u(s,X_s)^2\bigr]=d_*(s).$$ Equivalently, since $d_*(s)=A(s)+s\alpha(s)$, $$\label{eq:scalar-stationarity}
       \mathbb E\chi(s,X_s)=A(s)+\alpha(s)(s-\Gamma(s))
           \qquad(0\le s\le1).$$ In particular $$\label{static-eq:scalar-susceptibility}
       \chi(0,0)=A(0),\qquad \beta A(0)\le1.$$ Furthermore $\alpha(s)>0$ for every $s\in(0,1]$, $A(h)<A(0)$ for every $h\in(0,1]$, and $d_*(0+)=A(0)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-static-prop-scalar-minimizer"><summary>Proof of stmt-static-prop-scalar-minimizer</summary>

*Proof.* **Existence and variational support condition.** Weak convergence of probability measures on $[0,1]$ implies pointwise convergence of their distribution functions at continuity points of the limit. Bounded convergence then gives convergence in $L^1$. By Lemma <a href="#static-lem:scalar-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:scalar-regularity">9</a>, the functional $\mathcal P_\beta$ is continuous for this topology. The space of probability measures on $[0,1]$ is compact, so a minimizer exists.

Fix any minimizer and define $$G(q)=\int_q^1(\Gamma(s)-s)\,\mathrm ds.$$ Fubini’s theorem and Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-mass-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-mass-variation">[static-eq:scalar-mass-variation]</a> give $\int G\,\mathrm d\widetilde\mu\ge\int G\,\mathrm d\mu$ for every probability measure $\widetilde\mu$. Testing with point masses shows that $\mu$ is supported on the set of global minima of $G$. The function $G$ is twice continuously differentiable and satisfies $$G'(q)=q-\Gamma(q),\qquad G''(q)=1-\Gamma'(q).$$ The first- and second-derivative tests now give the claimed identities and inequalities at interior support points. At the right endpoint, $\Gamma(1)=\mathbb E\tanh^2(X_1)<1$, and hence $G'(1)>0$. Thus $1$ does not minimize $G$. Its minimum set is compact, so $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu$ stays a positive distance below $1$.

**The left endpoint of the support.** Let $q_0=\min\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu$. We claim that $q_0=0$. Suppose instead that $q_0\in(0,1)$. Then $\alpha=0$ on $[0,q_0)$, so $X_s=\beta B_s$ there and the equation for $\chi$ is the backward heat equation. Consequently $\chi(s,X_s)$ is a bounded martingale on $0\le s\le q_0$. One can see this either from the heat semigroup or by choosing step approximations identically zero below $q_0$. Its second moment is nondecreasing, as is $\Gamma'(s)$. But $\Gamma(q_0)=q_0$, $\Gamma(0)=0$, and $\Gamma'(q_0)\le1$. Thus the average of $\Gamma'$ on this interval is $1$, whereas its maximum is at most $1$. Continuity forces $\Gamma'\equiv1$ on $[0,q_0]$.

To contradict this constancy, observe that $\chi(q_0,\cdot)$ is positive everywhere and cannot be constant: otherwise $u(q_0,\cdot)$ would have a positive constant derivative, contrary to $|u|\le1$. The function $\chi(q_0,\cdot)$ is continuous, and $X_{q_0}$ is a nondegenerate Gaussian with full support. Therefore $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(\chi(q_0,X_{q_0}))>0$, while $\chi(0,X_0)$ is deterministic. The martingale’s second moment strictly increases between these endpoints, which contradicts the constancy of $\Gamma'$. Hence $q_0=0$. At this endpoint, $G$ has a minimum and symmetry gives $G'(0)=0$. The one-sided second-derivative test then yields $\Gamma'(0)\le1$.

**Stationarity with atoms included.** We have located the support and established $\Gamma(t)=t$ on it. It remains to turn these support identities into an identity at every time. Use Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-chi-expectation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-chi-expectation">[static-eq:scalar-chi-expectation]</a>, the terminal relation $\chi(1,z)=1-u(1,z)^2$, and Equation <a href="#static-eq:gamma-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:gamma-derivative">[static-eq:gamma-derivative]</a> to write $$\mathbb E\chi(s,X_s)=1-\Gamma(1)+\int_s^1\alpha(t)\Gamma'(t)\,\mathrm dt.$$ Stieltjes integration by parts with the right-continuous $\alpha$ gives the following formula, with every atom retained: $$\int_s^1\alpha(t)\Gamma'(t)\,\mathrm dt
 =\Gamma(1)-\alpha(s)\Gamma(s)
     -\int_{(s,1]}\Gamma(t)\,\mu(\,\mathrm dt).$$ In particular, the integral uses $(s,1]$, while the endpoint term uses $\alpha(s)$. Substituting $\Gamma(t)=t$ on $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu$, including at zero, proves Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-stationarity-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-stationarity-identity">[static-eq:scalar-stationarity-identity]</a>. Fubini’s theorem gives the expression for $A$ in Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-A-d-definitions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-A-d-definitions">[static-eq:scalar-A-d-definitions]</a>. At $s=0$ the stationarity identity becomes $\chi(0,0)=A(0)$. Finally, $\Gamma'(0)=\beta^2\chi(0,0)^2\le1$ proves Equation <a href="#static-eq:scalar-susceptibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-susceptibility">[static-eq:scalar-susceptibility]</a>.

Because $0\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\mu$, each interval $[0,s]$ with $s>0$ has positive $\mu$-mass. Thus $A(0)-A(h)=\int_0^h\alpha(s)\,\mathrm ds>0$ for every $h\in(0,1]$. The identity $d_*(s)=A(s)+s\alpha(s)$, together with continuity of $A$ at zero, also gives $d_*(0+)=A(0)$. ◻

</details>

For $\beta>1$, the susceptibility bound excludes the measure $\delta_0$. This makes the temperature derivative at a fixed minimizer strictly smaller than $\beta/2$. A finite-volume secant estimate transfers that strict inequality to the equilibrium energy.

<a id="stmt-static-lem-energy-gap"></a>
<div id="static-lem:energy-gap" class="lemma">

<a id="static-eq:energy-gap"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Strict bound on the equilibrium energy). *For each fixed $\beta>1$, there are $e_0=e_0(\beta)<\beta/2$ and $N=N(\beta)$ such that $$\label{static-eq:energy-gap}
 p_n'(\beta)=\frac1n\mathbb E\pi_{n,\beta}^J(H_n^J)\le e_0
 \qquad(n\ge N).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-static-lem-energy-gap"><summary>Proof of stmt-static-lem-energy-gap</summary>

<a id="static-eq:fixed-minimizer-temperature-derivative"></a>
*Proof.* Choose a minimizing measure $\mu$ at the specified $\beta$ and hold that measure fixed during the temperature variation. Combine Equations <a href="#static-eq:scalar-temperature-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-temperature-variation">[static-eq:scalar-temperature-variation]</a> and <a href="#static-eq:scalar-stationarity-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-stationarity-identity">[static-eq:scalar-stationarity-identity]</a>, and differentiate the penalty, to obtain $$\label{static-eq:fixed-minimizer-temperature-derivative}
 \left.\frac{\,\mathrm d}{\,\mathrm d\gamma}\mathcal P_\gamma(\mu)\right|_{\gamma=\beta}
 =\beta\int_0^1\bigl(d_*(s)-s\alpha(s)\bigr)\,\mathrm ds
 =\beta\int_0^1 A(s)\,\mathrm ds
 =\frac{\beta}{2}\left(1-\int_0^1t^2\,\mu(\,\mathrm dt)\right).$$ The last equality follows from two applications of Fubini’s theorem. If $\mu=\delta_0$, then $A(0)=1$, contradicting $\beta A(0)\le1$ for $\beta>1$. The second moment of $\mu$ is therefore positive. Denote the derivative in Equation <a href="#static-eq:fixed-minimizer-temperature-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:fixed-minimizer-temperature-derivative">[static-eq:fixed-minimizer-temperature-derivative]</a> by $L$; we have proved $L<\beta/2$.

Choose $e_0$ with $L<e_0<\beta/2$. Then fix $\varepsilon>0$ sufficiently small that $$\frac{\mathcal P_{\beta+\varepsilon}(\mu)-\mathcal P_\beta(\mu)}{\varepsilon}<e_0.$$ For every $n$, the finite-volume pressure is differentiable and convex in $\beta$. Bound its derivative by the forward secant, use the scalar upper trial bound at $\beta+\varepsilon$, and apply Theorem <a href="#static-thm:finite-size" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-thm:finite-size">6</a> at $\beta$. This gives $$p_n'(\beta)
 \le\frac{p_n(\beta+\varepsilon)-p_n(\beta)}{\varepsilon}
 \le\frac{\mathcal P_{\beta+\varepsilon}(\mu)-P_\beta}{\varepsilon}
         +\frac{C_\beta}{\varepsilon}n^{-1/24}.$$ Since $\mathcal P_\beta(\mu)=P_\beta$, the fixed secant slope is strictly below $e_0$. Its strict margin absorbs the error once $n$ is sufficiently large. Finally, differentiate the finite partition sum and then take Gaussian expectation to obtain the energy identity in Equation <a href="#static-eq:energy-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:energy-gap">[static-eq:energy-gap]</a>. The integrable bound $\max_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^n}|H_n^J(\sigma)|$ justifies this passage. The argument has varied only the temperature in the functional of one fixed measure; it requires no differentiability of $P_\beta$ or of a choice of minimizer as a function of $\beta$. ◻

</details>

<a id="static-sec:locking"></a>
### Locking small overlaps across a large overlap

When two configurations have a positive overlap bounded away from zero, we will show that their small overlaps with a third configuration are close, including their signs. This is the fixed-scale estimate used by the crossing construction. Spin reversal will then give the absolute-overlap version needed in Appendix <a href="#fixed-sec:crossings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fixed-sec:crossings">10</a>.

<a id="static-eq:locking-scalar-identities"></a>
Throughout the section, fix $\beta>1$ and a minimizer $\mu$ of the scalar functional. We use the notation of Section <a href="#static-sec:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-sec:scalar">3</a> and write $\pi=\pi_{n,\beta}^J$. The scalar inputs are $$\label{static-eq:locking-scalar-identities}
\begin{gathered}
0\leq\chi\leq1,\qquad |u|\leq1,\qquad
|\chi(s,z)-\chi(s,z')|\leq C_\beta|z-z'|,\\
\mathbb E\chi(s,X_s)=A(s)+\alpha(s)\bigl(s-\Gamma(s)\bigr),\qquad
\mathbb E\bigl[\chi(s,X_s)+\alpha(s)u(s,X_s)^2\bigr]=d_*(s),\\
\beta A(0)\leq1,\qquad A(h)<A(0)\quad(0<h\leq1),\qquad
d_*(s)\longrightarrow A(0)\quad(s\downarrow0).
\end{gathered}$$ We also use the scalar martingale evolution from Lemma <a href="#static-lem:scalar-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:scalar-regularity">9</a> and the bounds $\alpha(s)>0$ for $s>0$ and $0\le d_*(s)\le1$ from Proposition <a href="#static-prop:scalar-minimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-prop:scalar-minimizer">10</a>.

For a nonempty Gram constraint $D$, write $Z_{d,n}(D)$ for the unnormalized partition sum in <a href="#static-eq:constrained-pressure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:constrained-pressure">[static-eq:constrained-pressure]</a>. Thus $F_{d,n}(D)=n^{-1}\mathbb E\log Z_{d,n}(D)$, and division by $Z_{n,\beta}^d$ gives the probability of $D$ under $d$ independent Gibbs draws.

<a id="stmt-lem-fixed-locking"></a>
<div id="lem:fixed-locking" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:fixed-locking"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Signed fixed-scale locking). *Let $e_*=1/100$. For every fixed $h\in(0,1)$, there is $b>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n$, $$\label{eq:fixed-locking}
 \mathbb E\pi^{\otimes3}\!\left\{
 \begin{gathered}
 R(x,y)\ge h,\qquad |R(v,x)|,|R(v,y)|\le b,\\
 |R(v,x)-R(v,y)|>2n^{-e_*}
 \end{gathered}\right\}\le e^{-n^{9/10}}.$$ The same conclusion holds after decreasing $b$.*

</div>

We will prove a pressure deficit for each forbidden overlap matrix and then sum probability bounds. The conversion below will also apply to the shrinking gaps in Section <a href="#sec:narrow" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:narrow">7</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-pressure-prob"></a>
<div id="lem:pressure-prob" class="lemma">

<a id="static-eq:pressure-gap-probability"></a>
**Lemma 13** (From a pressure gap to a probability bound). *Fix a replica number $d$. If a feasible overlap matrix $D$ satisfies $$F_{d,n}(D)\le dP_\beta-\eta,
 \qquad \eta\ge2dC_\beta n^{-1/24},$$ where $C_\beta$ is the constant in Theorem <a href="#static-thm:finite-size" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-thm:finite-size">6</a>, then, for all sufficiently large $n$, $$\label{static-eq:pressure-gap-probability}
 \mathbb E\frac{Z_{d,n}(D)}{Z_{n,\beta}^d}
 \le \exp(-n\eta/4)+\exp(-c_{\beta,d}n\eta^2).$$ The constants are uniform over $D$ and over $n$-dependent gaps satisfying the displayed lower bound.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pressure-prob"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pressure-prob</summary>
