Visual witness description of PDF page 38 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A schematic illustration divided into an upper tree-branching diagram and two lower coordinate plots.
- Upper diagram: Titled "Increments used in $Y = aY_0 + \theta Y_1$". A horizontal line representing the "common branch" extends from the left to a branching point at coordinate $r$. At $r$, it bifurcates into two parallel horizontal paths extending rightward: an upper path labeled "$x, y\text{ branch}$" and a lower path labeled "$v\text{ branch}$". A horizontal coordinate axis below marks values $r - h$, $r$, $r + h$, and $q$, labeled "local scalar time". The interval $[r - h, r + h]$ along the $x, y$ branch is shaded in light blue, with label "$a/h$" above the sub-interval $[r - h, r]$ and label "$-a/h$" above $[r, r + h]$. The interval $[r, r + h]$ along the $v$ branch is shaded in light blue and labeled "$\theta/h$".
- Lower-left plot: Titled "Tent $T$ on the $x$ path". A horizontal axis has ticks at $r - h$, $r$, $r + h$, and $q$. A vertical tick marks height 1 at $r$. A piecewise-linear red curve forms a symmetric triangle (tent) starting at 0 at $r - h$, peaking at height 1 at $r$, and returning to 0 at $r + h$, remaining flat at 0 outside this interval.
- Lower-right plot: Titled "Ramp $S$ on the $v$ path". A horizontal axis has ticks at $r$ and $r + h$. A vertical tick marks height 1 at $r + h$. A red line segment starts at 0 at $r$ and ramps linearly up to height 1 at $r + h$.
```


At each level of the first two stages, add $\delta$ times that level’s contribution to $Y$ to the $x$ field, and subtract it from the $y$ field. Leave $v$ unchanged. Each modified increment is a deterministic linear image of its original Gaussian increment. Consequently its covariance is positive semidefinite, and increments at different levels remain independent. This gives an admissible path $\widetilde Q$ with the original masses. At the end of the second stage the added fields are $0,\delta Y,-\delta Y$.

<a id="eq:narrow-covariance-end"></a>
<a id="eq:narrow-norm-expansion"></a>
<a id="eq:narrow-mismatch"></a>
For any prefix, write $K_c$ for the accumulated raw variance of $Y$, divided by $\beta^2$, and write $g_v,g_x$ for its raw covariances with the base $v,x$ fields in the same units. Its covariance with the base $y$ field equals $g_x$. The coefficients on the three active intervals are $a/h,-a/h,\theta/h$, so $$\label{eq:narrow-covariance-end}
 K=\frac{2a^2+\theta^2}{h},\qquad
 g_v^{\rm end}=a+\theta=1,\qquad g_x^{\rm end}=a-a=0.$$ Let $e_v,e_x,e_y$ be the coordinate vectors and set $L_2=(e_x-e_y)(e_x-e_y)^{\mathsf T}$. At every prefix, <a id="eq:narrow-covariance-expansion"></a>$$\label{eq:narrow-covariance-expansion}
 \widetilde Q=Q+\delta L_1+\delta^2K_cL_2,
 \qquad
 L_1=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 0&g_v&-g_v\\
 g_v&2g_x&0\\
 -g_v&0&-2g_x
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ For the Frobenius product $U:V=\sum_{i,j}U_{ij}V_{ij}$, both $Q$ and $L_2$ are orthogonal to $L_1$. Also $|L_1|^2=4g_v^2+8g_x^2$ and $|L_2|^2=4$. Expanding gives $$\label{eq:narrow-norm-expansion}
 |\widetilde Q|^2-|Q|^2
 =\delta^2\bigl(4g_v^2+8g_x^2+2K_c\,Q:L_2\bigr)
   +4\delta^4K_c^2.$$ The endpoint is <a id="eq:narrow-modified-end"></a>$$\label{eq:narrow-modified-end}
 \widetilde Q_{\rm end}=D+\delta^2K L_2.$$ Its diagonal need not be one. We therefore use precisely the arbitrary-endpoint comparison <a href="#static-eq:matrix-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:matrix-bound">[static-eq:matrix-bound]</a>, proved in Proposition <a href="#static-prop:matrix-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-prop:matrix-bound">5</a>. It assigns the endpoint discrepancy the cost $$\label{eq:narrow-mismatch}
 \frac{\beta^2}{4}|D-\widetilde Q_{\rm end}|^2
 =\beta^2\delta^4K^2.$$

**Step 3. The penalty gain.** We next compute the quadratic terms in the interpolation penalty. The base $x,y$ fields coincide before the third stage, so $Q:L_2=0$ there. In the third stage, $Q:L_2=2(s-q)$ and $K_c=K$ is fixed. The term $2K_c\,Q:L_2$ in <a href="#eq:narrow-norm-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-norm-expansion">[eq:narrow-norm-expansion]</a> therefore contributes $\beta^2\delta^2K A(q)$ to the penalty.

<a id="eq:narrow-penalty"></a>
For the other quadratic terms put $$I=\int m\,\bigl(\,\mathrm d(g_v^2)+2\,\mathrm d(g_x^2)\bigr).$$ The three active pieces have separate roles. Before the split, on $[r-h,r]$, both covariances grow from $0$ to $a$ at mass $\alpha(s)/3$; this contributes $\alpha(r)a^2+O(\rho^4)$. On the active $v$ steps, $g_v$ grows from $a$ to $a+\theta$ and $g_x$ is fixed; this contributes $\alpha(r)(2a\theta+\theta^2)+O(\rho^4)$. On the active shared $x,y$ steps, $g_x$ decreases from $a$ to zero and $g_v$ is fixed. The factor $2$ in $I$ cancels the factor $1/2$ in their masses, giving $-\alpha(r)a^2+O(\rho^4)$. The error in replacing each mass by $\alpha(r)$ is controlled by <a href="#eq:narrow-step-oscillation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-step-oscillation">[eq:narrow-step-oscillation]</a> times the total variation of the relevant covariance square. These total variations are uniformly bounded, independently of the partition and its interleaving. Thus <a id="eq:narrow-I"></a>$$\label{eq:narrow-I}
 I=\alpha(r)(2a\theta+\theta^2)+O(\rho^4).$$ Finally, $K_c$ is a nondecreasing cumulative variance and all masses are nonnegative. The fourth-order contribution $\int m\,\,\mathrm d(K_c^2)$ is therefore nonnegative. Combining the terms proves $$\label{eq:narrow-penalty}
 \frac{\beta^2}{4}\int m\,
       \,\mathrm d\bigl(|\widetilde Q|^2-|Q|^2\bigr)
 \ge
 \beta^2\delta^2
 \bigl(KA(q)+\alpha(r)(2a\theta+\theta^2)-C\rho^4\bigr).$$

<a id="eq:narrow-recursive-comparison"></a>
**Step 4. The recursive-value cost.** Let $\widetilde\Psi$ denote the modified one-site value. Integrating the unchanged third stage backwards leaves the boundary difference <a id="eq:narrow-U"></a>$$\label{eq:narrow-U}
 U=\Phi(q,X_q^x+\delta Y)+\Phi(q,X_q^x-\delta Y)
                -2\Phi(q,X_q^x).$$ Initially view this as a function of the full raw Gaussian prefix through the second stage. Let $\mathbb E_0$ denote expectation under its base tilted law. We claim that $$\label{eq:narrow-recursive-comparison}
 \widetilde\Psi-\Psi_0\le\log\mathbb E_0 e^U.$$

Apply the normalized-kernel comparison <a href="#static-eq:locking-recursive-sandwich" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:locking-recursive-sandwich">[static-eq:locking-recursive-sandwich]</a> with boundary change $U$. Here the state at each level is the entire raw Gaussian prefix: $Y$ uses several increments, so the current field alone need not determine the modified future value. If $K_i$ is the normalized base kernel and $\Delta_i$ is the change in recursive value, the ratio of the defining integrals still gives $$\Delta_i=\frac1{m_i}\log K_i e^{m_i\Delta_{i+1}}
 \quad(m_i>0),\qquad
 \Delta_i=K_i\Delta_{i+1}\quad(m_i=0).$$ The kernel induction used for <a href="#static-eq:locking-recursive-sandwich" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:locking-recursive-sandwich">[static-eq:locking-recursive-sandwich]</a> therefore applies without change and proves <a href="#eq:narrow-recursive-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-recursive-comparison">[eq:narrow-recursive-comparison]</a>. This argument uses no inverse transformation of the fields, so singular covariance increments cause no difficulty. Every kernel is from the base hierarchy, whose tilted prefix law was identified in Step 1.

<a id="eq:narrow-U-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:narrow-exponential-error"></a>
Write $\chi_q=\chi(q,X_q^x)$. By Lemma <a href="#static-lem:scalar-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:scalar-regularity">9</a>, $$\label{eq:narrow-U-bounds}
 0\le U\le\delta^2Y^2,\qquad
 |U-\delta^2\chi_qY^2|\le C_\beta|\delta Y|^3.$$ The diffusion representation under $\mathbb E_0$ writes $Y=G+B_*$, where $G$ is a centered Gaussian linear combination of Brownian increments with variance $\beta^2K$, and $|B_*|\le C_\beta$. Indeed, each drift in <a href="#eq:narrow-Y" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-Y">[eq:narrow-Y]</a> is bounded by $\beta^2$, integrated for time $h$, and then divided by $h$. No independence between $G$ and $B_*$ is required. Since $\delta^2/h=O(\rho^{32})$, Gaussian exponential moments and $|Y|\le|G|+C_\beta$ imply, for all large $n$, $$\mathbb E_0|Y|^3\le C_\beta h^{-3/2},\qquad
 \mathbb E_0\!\left[Y^4e^{\delta^2Y^2}\right]\le C_\beta h^{-2}.$$ Use $e^u\le1+u+\tfrac12u^2e^u$ for $u\ge0$, followed by $\log(1+z)\le z$, and then <a href="#eq:narrow-U-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-U-bounds">[eq:narrow-U-bounds]</a>. We obtain $$\label{eq:narrow-exponential-error}
 \log\mathbb E_0e^U
 \le \delta^2\mathbb E_0[\chi_qY^2]
      +C_\beta\bigl(|\delta|^3h^{-3/2}+\delta^4h^{-2}\bigr).$$

We have now reduced the comparison to estimating $\mathbb E_0[\chi_qY^2]$. Its Gaussian variance term will cancel the $KA(q)$ part of the penalty up to the pair deviation. The strict gain must come from the mixed moment of $Y_0$ and $Y_1$.

<a id="eq:narrow-quadratic"></a>
<a id="eq:narrow-density"></a>
**Step 5. The tent on the first branch.** We will establish the quadratic-moment bound $$\label{eq:narrow-quadratic}
 \mathbb E_0[\chi_qY^2]
 \le\beta^2K\,\mathbb E_0\chi_q
    +2a\theta\beta^4\alpha(r)\mathbb E_0[\chi_q\chi_r]
    +C_\beta(\rho^4+\theta^2),
 \qquad \chi_r=\chi(r,X_r^x).$$ Begin with the scalar $x$ path through $q$. Write its partition as $0=s_0<\cdots<s_N=q$ and its scalar masses as $m_j^{\rm sc}=\alpha(s_j)$. Its normalized tilted density $D_x$ relative to the raw Gaussian increments has logarithm $$\label{eq:narrow-density}
 \ell:=\log D_x
 =\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}m_j^{\rm sc}
    \bigl(\Phi(s_{j+1},X^x_{s_{j+1}})
                  -\Phi(s_j,X^x_{s_j})\bigr).$$ The scalar masses appear here because the base-law factorization in Step 1 has already canceled the divided matrix masses.

<a id="eq:narrow-tent-ibp"></a>
Let $T$ be the continuous piecewise linear tent supported on $[r-h,r+h]$, zero at both endpoints and equal to one at $r$. For functions of the raw cumulative coordinates, denote by $\partial$ the derivative obtained by replacing $X^x_s$ with $X^x_s+tT(s)$ and differentiating at $t=0$. Under the raw law, $${\rm Cov}_{\rm raw}(Y_0,X^x_s)=\beta^2T(s),\qquad
 {\rm Var}_{\rm raw}(Y_0)=\frac{2\beta^2}{h}.$$ Finite-dimensional Gaussian integration by parts gives $$\label{eq:narrow-tent-ibp}
 \mathbb E_{\rm raw}[Y_0f]=\beta^2\mathbb E_{\rm raw}[\partial f],\qquad
 \mathbb E_{\rm raw}[Y_0^2f]
 =\frac{2\beta^2}{h}\mathbb E_{\rm raw}f
      +\beta^4\mathbb E_{\rm raw}[\partial^2f].$$ For a direct verification, multiply the Gaussian density by $\exp\{tY_0-t^2(2\beta^2/h)/2\}$. This shifts every cumulative coordinate by $t\beta^2T(s)$; one and two differentiations give the two formulas. These derivatives act on raw coordinates, not on the solution map of the tilted stochastic differential equation.

Telescope <a href="#eq:narrow-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-density">[eq:narrow-density]</a>. Since $T(0)=T(q)=0$, only internal coefficients $m_{j-1}^{\rm sc}-m_j^{\rm sc}$ remain: $$\partial\ell
 =\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}(m_{j-1}^{\rm sc}-m_j^{\rm sc})
          T(s_j)u(s_j,X^x_{s_j}),\qquad
 \partial^2\ell
 =\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}(m_{j-1}^{\rm sc}-m_j^{\rm sc})
          T(s_j)^2\chi(s_j,X^x_{s_j}).$$ Only points strictly between $r-h$ and $r+h$ contribute. The sum of the absolute coefficients at those points is at most $\alpha(r+h)-\alpha(r-h)$. Thus <a id="eq:narrow-density-derivatives"></a>$$\label{eq:narrow-density-derivatives}
 |\partial\ell|\le\rho^4,\qquad
 |\partial^2\ell|\le\rho^4.$$ This estimate includes an atom at $r$. Atoms at $r-h$ or $r+h$ contribute zero because the tent vanishes there. No continuity of $\alpha$ at these points is assumed.

<a id="eq:narrow-Y0-square"></a>
<a id="eq:narrow-Y0-linear"></a>
The endpoint $q$ lies outside the tent, so $\partial\chi_q=0$. Apply the second identity of <a href="#eq:narrow-tent-ibp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-tent-ibp">[eq:narrow-tent-ibp]</a> to $f=D_x\chi_q$: $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_0[\chi_qY_0^2]
 &=\frac{2\beta^2}{h}\mathbb E_0\chi_q
   +\beta^4\mathbb E_0\!\left[
         \chi_q\bigl((\partial\ell)^2+\partial^2\ell\bigr)\right]
 \notag\\
 &=\frac{2\beta^2}{h}\mathbb E_0\chi_q+O_\beta(\rho^4).
 \label{eq:narrow-Y0-square}
\end{aligned}$$ At the tent’s peak, put $u_r=u(r,X_r^x)$; then $\partial u_r=\chi_r$. The first identity applied to $f=D_x\chi_qu_r$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_0[\chi_qY_0u_r]
 &=\beta^2\mathbb E_0[\chi_q\chi_r]
       +\beta^2\mathbb E_0[\chi_qu_r\partial\ell]
 \notag\\
 &=\beta^2\mathbb E_0[\chi_q\chi_r]+O_\beta(\rho^4).
 \label{eq:narrow-Y0-linear}
\end{aligned}$$ Both integrations by parts are legitimate for every fixed finite partition. Scalar values grow at most linearly, their needed derivatives are bounded, and the density grows at most exponential-linearly in the finite Gaussian vector. Gaussian integrability therefore justifies differentiation directly, or after truncation and passage to the limit. The error estimates above are uniform over the partitions.

**Step 6. The ramp on the other branch and the mixed moment.** Condition now on the entire path $X^x$ through $q$. The product law from Step 1 leaves the future $v$ branch as a scalar diffusion starting at the fixed value $X_r$. On $[r,r+h]$, its conditional tilted density has the form <a href="#eq:narrow-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-density">[eq:narrow-density]</a>, with starting time $r$. Use the ramp $S(s)=(s-r)/h$ and denote the corresponding raw-coordinate derivative by $\partial_v$. The raw covariance of $Y_1$ with the cumulative coordinate at $s$ is $\beta^2S(s)$, and its raw variance is $\beta^2/h$.

<a id="eq:narrow-Y1-square"></a>
Let $\ell_v$ be this branch’s conditional log density. Telescoping its derivative produces the terminal term $m_{\rm last}u(r+h,X^v_{r+h})$ and internal terms with total absolute coefficient at most $m_{\rm last}-m_{\rm first}\le1$. The initial value is fixed and the ramp is zero there. Using $|u|\le1$ and $\chi\le1$ gives $$|\partial_v\ell_v|\le2,\qquad
 |\partial_v^2\ell_v|\le2.$$ The conditional second Gaussian integration-by-parts identity therefore yields $$\label{eq:narrow-Y1-square}
 \mathbb E_0[Y_1^2\mid X^x]
 =\frac{\beta^2}{h}
  +\beta^4\mathbb E_0\!\left[
       (\partial_v\ell_v)^2+\partial_v^2\ell_v\mid X^x\right]
 =\frac{\beta^2}{h}+O_\beta(1).$$ The error is uniform in the starting field $X_r$. Multiplying by the $X^x$-measurable variable $\chi_q$ and averaging gives $$\mathbb E_0[\chi_qY_1^2]
 =\frac{\beta^2}{h}\mathbb E_0\chi_q+O_\beta(1).$$

<a id="eq:narrow-Y1-mean"></a>
<a id="eq:narrow-mixed"></a>
For the mixed moment we need the conditional first moment of $Y_1$. The Brownian increment has conditional mean zero. The arbitrary-start martingale identity <a href="#static-eq:scalar-martingale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:scalar-martingale">[static-eq:scalar-martingale]</a> gives $\mathbb E_0[u(z,X_z^v)\mid X^x]=u_r$, since the independent branch starts at $X_r$. Hence its drift representation gives exactly $$\label{eq:narrow-Y1-mean}
 \mathbb E_0[Y_1\mid X^x]
 =\frac{\beta^2}{h}\int_r^{r+h}
         \alpha(z)\mathbb E_0[u(z,X^v_z)\mid X^x]\,\,\mathrm dz
 =\beta^2\bar\alpha\,u_r,\qquad
 \bar\alpha=\frac1h\int_r^{r+h}\alpha(z)\,\,\mathrm dz.$$ Conditioning on the whole $x$ path introduces no additional conditioning on the $v$ branch’s future noise. Moreover, <a href="#eq:narrow-step-oscillation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-step-oscillation">[eq:narrow-step-oscillation]</a> implies $|\bar\alpha-\alpha(r)|\le\rho^4$. Since $Y_0,\chi_q,u_r$ are $X^x$-measurable, combine <a href="#eq:narrow-Y0-linear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-Y0-linear">[eq:narrow-Y0-linear]</a> with <a href="#eq:narrow-Y1-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-Y1-mean">[eq:narrow-Y1-mean]</a> to obtain $$\label{eq:narrow-mixed}
 \mathbb E_0[\chi_qY_0Y_1]
 =\beta^2\bar\alpha\,\mathbb E_0[\chi_qY_0u_r]
 =\beta^4\alpha(r)\mathbb E_0[\chi_q\chi_r]+O_\beta(\rho^4).$$ Finally expand $Y^2=a^2Y_0^2+2a\theta Y_0Y_1+\theta^2Y_1^2$. Equations <a href="#eq:narrow-covariance-end" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-covariance-end">[eq:narrow-covariance-end]</a>, <a href="#eq:narrow-Y0-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-Y0-square">[eq:narrow-Y0-square]</a>, <a href="#eq:narrow-Y1-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-Y1-square">[eq:narrow-Y1-square]</a>, and <a href="#eq:narrow-mixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-mixed">[eq:narrow-mixed]</a> prove <a href="#eq:narrow-quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-quadratic">[eq:narrow-quadratic]</a>. The distinction between the two errors matters: the tent contributes $O_\beta(\rho^4)$, while the ramp’s $O_\beta(1)$ error is multiplied by $\theta^2$.

<a id="eq:narrow-step-pressure"></a>
**Step 7. Passing the pressure comparison to the minimizer.** We can now apply <a href="#static-eq:matrix-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:matrix-bound">[static-eq:matrix-bound]</a> to the modified finite hierarchy. Combine the base value, mismatch, penalty, recursive comparison, and moment estimates in <a href="#eq:narrow-base-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-base-value">[eq:narrow-base-value]</a>, <a href="#eq:narrow-mismatch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-mismatch">[eq:narrow-mismatch]</a>, <a href="#eq:narrow-penalty" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-penalty">[eq:narrow-penalty]</a>, <a href="#eq:narrow-recursive-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-recursive-comparison">[eq:narrow-recursive-comparison]</a>, <a href="#eq:narrow-exponential-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-exponential-error">[eq:narrow-exponential-error]</a>, and <a href="#eq:narrow-quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-quadratic">[eq:narrow-quadratic]</a>. Since $K=O(h^{-1})$, the endpoint cost is part of the $\delta^4h^{-2}$ error. Discard the additional nonpositive term $-\beta^2\delta^2\alpha(r)\theta^2$ to get $$\begin{aligned}
 F_{3,n}(D)\le 3\mathcal P_\beta(\alpha)
 +\delta^2\bigl\{&
   \beta^2K(\mathbb E_0\chi_q-A(q))
   +2a\theta\beta^2\alpha(r)
                    (\beta^2\mathbb E_0[\chi_q\chi_r]-1)
   +C_\beta(\rho^4+\theta^2)\bigr\}
 \notag\\
 &\hspace{-1em}
   +C_\beta\bigl(|\delta|^3h^{-3/2}+\delta^4h^{-2}\bigr).
 \label{eq:narrow-step-pressure}
\end{aligned}$$ The constants do not depend on the partition.

Keep $n,r,q,\delta$ fixed and send the mesh size to zero. Lemma <a href="#static-lem:scalar-regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:scalar-regularity">9</a> gives uniform convergence of the scalar derivatives and $L^1$ stability of the values. Couple the diffusions with the same Brownian motion. Their drifts are uniformly bounded and Lipschitz in space, so $L^1$ convergence of $\alpha$ together with uniform convergence of $u$ gives path convergence by Gronwall’s inequality. The bounded expectations in <a href="#eq:narrow-step-pressure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-step-pressure">[eq:narrow-step-pressure]</a> consequently converge. So do $A(q)$ and the scalar functional, the latter to $P_\beta$. By construction, every approximation uses the actual value $\alpha(r)$.

<a id="eq:narrow-pressure"></a>
This limit is taken at fixed $h$, so it requires no approximation rate uniform as $h\downarrow0$. Returning to the minimizing data, and writing $\mathbb E$ for its scalar-process expectation, we have $$\begin{aligned}
 F_{3,n}(D)\le 3P_\beta
 +\delta^2\bigl\{&
   \beta^2K(\mathbb E\chi_q-A(q))
   +2a\theta\beta^2\alpha(r)
                    (\beta^2\mathbb E[\chi_q\chi_r]-1)
   +C_\beta(\rho^4+\theta^2)\bigr\}
 \notag\\
 &\hspace{-1em}
   +C_\beta\bigl(|\delta|^3h^{-3/2}+\delta^4h^{-2}\bigr).
 \label{eq:narrow-pressure}
\end{aligned}$$ In the product $\chi_q\chi_r$, both susceptibilities belong to the same scalar path. It remains to show that their evolution makes the quadratic coefficient negative by more than all displayed errors.

<a id="eq:narrow-susceptibility-loss"></a>
**Step 8. The strict quadratic margin and the scale choices.** The assumed identity <a href="#eq:narrow-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-identity">[eq:narrow-identity]</a> and the scalar evolution imply $$\beta^2\mathbb E\chi_r^2=\Gamma'(r)=1.$$ Multiply the conditional susceptibility identity <a href="#eq:scalar-evolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-evolution">[eq:scalar-evolution]</a> by $\chi_r$ and average. It gives $$\label{eq:narrow-susceptibility-loss}
 \beta^2\mathbb E[\chi_q\chi_r]
 =1-\beta^4\int_r^q\alpha(z)\mathbb E[\chi_z^2\chi_r]\,\,\mathrm dz
 \le1-c(q-r)\le1-c\rho$$ with a uniform $c>0$ over the allowed $r,q$. To justify uniformity, including $q=1$, use the drift bound to obtain $\sup_{s\le1}|X_s|\le\beta\sup_{s\le1}|B_s|+\beta^2$. Choose a fixed $M$ so that the event that the right side is at most $M$ has positive probability $p_M$. Continuity and strict positivity of $\chi$ imply $$c_M:=\min_{0\le s\le1,\ |z|\le M}\chi(s,z)>0.$$ Thus $\mathbb E[\chi_z^2\chi_r]\ge p_Mc_M^3$ for all the times under consideration. Also $\alpha(z)\ge\alpha(t_0/2)>0$ when $z\ge r$. We may take $c=\beta^4\alpha(t_0/2)p_Mc_M^3$ in <a href="#eq:narrow-susceptibility-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-susceptibility-loss">[eq:narrow-susceptibility-loss]</a>. This argument uses positivity on a compact set, not a lower bound for $\chi$ on the whole real line.

<a id="eq:narrow-pressure-gap"></a>
The all-time stationarity identity <a href="#eq:scalar-stationarity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-stationarity">[eq:scalar-stationarity]</a> and the pair restriction <a href="#eq:narrow-pair-deviation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-pair-deviation">[eq:narrow-pair-deviation]</a> give $$\mathbb E\chi_q-A(q)=\alpha(q)(q-\Gamma(q))\le\rho^{13}.$$ Since $K=O(\rho^{-8})$, the first term in braces in <a href="#eq:narrow-pressure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-pressure">[eq:narrow-pressure]</a> is at most $C_\beta\rho^5$. For all large $n$, $a\ge1/2$; using $\theta=\rho^2$, $\alpha(r)\ge\alpha(t_0/2)>0$, and <a href="#eq:narrow-susceptibility-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-susceptibility-loss">[eq:narrow-susceptibility-loss]</a>, the second term is at most $-c_1\rho^3$. The remaining brace term is $O_\beta(\rho^4)$. The last two errors, after division by $\delta^2$, satisfy $$C_\beta|\delta|h^{-3/2}\le C_\beta\rho^8,
 \qquad
 C_\beta\delta^2h^{-2}\le C_\beta\rho^{24}.$$ Every positive error is therefore smaller than the negative $\rho^3$ contribution for all large $n$. Uniformly over the feasible matrices in question, this proves $$\label{eq:narrow-pressure-gap}
 F_{3,n}(D)\le3P_\beta-c_2\delta^2\rho^3
             \le3P_\beta-c_2\rho^{43}.$$

**Step 9. From pressure deficits to the claimed probability.** The choice $\kappa=1/10000$ satisfies $43\kappa<1/24$, so the deficit in <a href="#eq:narrow-pressure-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-pressure-gap">[eq:narrow-pressure-gap]</a> dominates the finite-size error required by Lemma <a href="#lem:pressure-prob" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pressure-prob">13</a>. For each feasible triple matrix that lemma gives two exponential bounds, with exponents at least constant multiples of $n\rho^{43}$ and $n\rho^{86}$. The latter equals $n^{1-86\kappa}=n^{0.9914}$. Sum these bounds over the at most $(n+1)^3$ triple overlap matrices.

For the pairs removed in <a href="#eq:narrow-pair-deviation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-pair-deviation">[eq:narrow-pair-deviation]</a>, <a href="#static-eq:pair-pressure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:pair-pressure">[static-eq:pair-pressure]</a> and the same lemma give exponents at least constant multiples of $n\rho^{26}$ and $n\rho^{52}=n^{0.9948}$. There are at most $n+1$ pair overlaps to sum over. The fixed constants and polynomial counts in both sums are absorbed by $\exp(-n^{4/5})$ for all sufficiently large $n$. This proves <a href="#eq:narrow-locking-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-locking-probability">[eq:narrow-locking-probability]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:dynamics"></a>
### Reference banks, crossings and slow mixing

We have established all static inputs. It remains to use them along a path: coverage selects references, fixed-scale locking controls incompatible references, and narrow-annulus locking preserves constraints during backwards searches. We first reduce Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> to a sign passage, then construct the required references, and finally bound the probability of all resulting threshold constraints. As before, $L=n^\kappa$, $\rho=n^{-\kappa}$, and constants may depend on the fixed inverse temperature but not on $n$.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Stationary paths and a sign-passage criterion

<a id="eq:dyn-poisson-tail"></a>
Given $J$, start the discrete heat-bath chain $(x_k)_{k\ge0}$ at $x_0\sim\pi^J$. Each step chooses a uniform site and resamples its spin from the conditional Gibbs law. Stationarity gives marginal $\pi^J$ at every deterministic index. If $N$ is an independent rate-$n$ Poisson process, $x_{N(t)}$ has conditional transition kernel $P_t^J$ from the realized $x_0$. We work up to the deterministic index <a id="eq:dyn-time-horizon"></a>$$\label{eq:dyn-time-horizon}
 m=\lceil e^{2L}\rceil.$$ Markov’s inequality gives $$\label{eq:dyn-poisson-tail}
 \mathbb P\{N(e^L)>m\}\leq \frac{ne^L}{m}\leq ne^{-L}=o(1).$$ For every fixed configuration $v$, the overlap $R(v,x_k)$ changes by at most $2/n$ at a single step.

A *bank* is an ordered list of independent $\pi^J$ samples. Given $J$, all raw bank entries are mutually independent and independent of the complete path. Their sizes are deterministic functions of $n$. A chosen reference may be reversed in sign at selection; that orientation is thereafter fixed.

<a id="eq:dyn-tv-bridge"></a>
Suppose a first bank is used to select a reference $v_1$ satisfying $R(v_1,x_0)\geq q_*$, with the choice and orientation depending only on $J,x_0$ and this bank. Write $\mathcal S$ for successful selection, and define its sign-passage event by $$\mathcal A=\mathcal S\cap
 \{R(v_1,x_k)\leq0\text{ for some }0\leq k\leq m\}.$$ Also write $$\mathcal M=\left\{
 \lVert P_{e^L}^J(x_0,\cdot)-\pi^J\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\frac14\right\}.$$ Spin-flip symmetry implies, for every oriented $v$, $$\pi^J\{z:R(v,z)\leq0\}\geq\frac12.$$ Condition on $J,x_0$ and successful first-bank selection. Since that selection uses no future updates, the path and clock still have their original conditional laws. On $\mathcal M$, total variation therefore puts conditional probability at least $1/4$ on the opposite-sign half-space at time $e^L$. Removing clock overruns gives $$\label{eq:dyn-tv-bridge}
 \mathbb P(\mathcal A)\geq
 \frac14\mathbb P(\mathcal M\cap\mathcal S)
 -\mathbb P\{N(e^L)>m\}.$$ Thus the remaining task is concrete: make the first reference available with probability tending to one, and make its sign passage have probability tending to zero.

<a id="eq:dyn-t0-choice"></a>
Choose a fixed $b\in(0,1)$ no larger than the small-overlap cutoff in Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-locking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-locking">12</a> with large-overlap cutoff $q_*$. Next choose $b'\in(0,b)$ no larger than that lemma’s small-overlap cutoff with large-overlap cutoff $b$. Fix $t_0>0$ so small that $$\label{eq:dyn-t0-choice}
 4t_0<\min\{q_*,b',b^2/2\}.$$ We use the notation <a id="eq:dyn-fixed-error"></a>$$\label{eq:dyn-fixed-error}
 \eta_n=n^{-1/100}=\rho^{100}$$ for the scale in fixed-scale locking.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The case with a fixed forbidden overlap interval

<a id="eq:dyn-fixed-forbidden"></a>
There are two possible scalar regimes. In the first, $\Gamma(z)=z$ fails at some point of $(t_0/2,3t_0)$. Continuity then supplies fixed constants $$t_0/2<a_0<a_1<3t_0,\qquad d_0>0,$$ such that $|q-\Gamma(q)|\geq d_0$ for all $q\in[a_0,a_1]$. The pair gap <a href="#static-eq:pair-pressure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:pair-pressure">[static-eq:pair-pressure]</a>, Lemma <a href="#lem:pressure-prob" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pressure-prob">13</a>, and the polynomial number of feasible pair overlaps imply, for some $c>0$ and all sufficiently large $n$, $$\label{eq:dyn-fixed-forbidden}
 \mathbb E\big[(\pi^J)^{\otimes2}
       \{(v,x):R(v,x)\in[a_0,a_1]\}\big]\leq e^{-cn}.$$

Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:coverage">18</a> with $D_1=3$, let $D_3$ be its constant, and take a single bank with $K=\lceil\exp((D_3+1)L)\rceil$ entries. Select its first entry with $|R(v,x_0)|\geq q_*$, oriented to give positive overlap. On the disorder event of coverage for the whole configuration space, the Gibbs mass of starting states whose qualifying neighborhood has mass below $e^{-D_3L}$ is at most $e^{-3L}$. For every other starting state, the independent bank misses that neighborhood with probability at most $e^{-e^L}$. Hence $\mathbb P(\mathcal S)\to1$.

At a deterministic $k\le m$, the path point and a fixed signed bank entry are independent Gibbs draws conditional on $J$. Sum <a href="#eq:dyn-fixed-forbidden" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-fixed-forbidden">[eq:dyn-fixed-forbidden]</a> over entries, signs and indices: $$\mathbb P\{R(\sigma v,x_k)\in[a_0,a_1]
     \text{ for some bank entry }v,\ \sigma\in\{-1,1\},\ k\leq m\}
 \leq 2K(m+1)e^{-cn}=o(1).$$ For large $n$, a sequence beginning at overlap at least $q_*>a_1$ and reaching nonpositive overlap, with jumps at most $2/n<a_1-a_0$, must visit $[a_0,a_1]$. Therefore $\mathbb P(\mathcal A)\to0$. Together with <a href="#eq:dyn-tv-bridge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-tv-bridge">[eq:dyn-tv-bridge]</a>, this proves the theorem in this case.

<a id="eq:dyn-identity-interval"></a>
This completes the forbidden-interval case. In the second regime we have $$\label{eq:dyn-identity-interval}
 \Gamma(z)=z\qquad(t_0/2<z<3t_0),$$ and put <a id="eq:dyn-local-scales"></a>$$\label{eq:dyn-local-scales}
 h=\rho^8,\qquad \varepsilon=\rho^{20}.$$

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Thresholds and simultaneous bank properties

<a id="eq:dyn-threshold-separation"></a>
Define the fixed integers and the number of levels by <a id="eq:dyn-block-parameters"></a>$$\label{eq:dyn-block-parameters}
 H_0=\lceil 2/b^2\rceil,\qquad B=H_0+2,\qquad
 p=B\left\lfloor\frac{t_0}{5B\rho}\right\rfloor.$$ For large $n$, $p\ge B$. Divide the $p$ levels into consecutive blocks of $B$ levels each. At the first level of every block after the first, we *reset* the selection cell to the whole space. Older retained references are kept unless they meet the conflict rule specified below. Independently of the disorder, banks and path, draw independent thresholds $r_i$, uniform on <a id="eq:dyn-threshold-intervals"></a>$$\label{eq:dyn-threshold-intervals}
 I_i=[t_0+4(i-1)\rho,\ t_0+(4(i-1)+1)\rho].$$ These intervals lie in $[t_0,2t_0]$, and $$\label{eq:dyn-threshold-separation}
 r_j-r_i\geq3\rho\qquad(i<j).$$ Fubini’s theorem gives $$\mathbb E\big[\mu([r_i-2h,r_i+2h])\big]\leq\frac{4h}{\rho}.$$ Consequently Markov’s inequality and a union bound show that the event <a id="eq:dyn-threshold-good"></a>$$\label{eq:dyn-threshold-good}
 \mathcal T=\{\mu([r_i-2h,r_i+2h])\leq\rho^4
                   \text{ for every }1\leq i\leq p\}$$ satisfies <a id="eq:dyn-threshold-good-prob"></a>$$\label{eq:dyn-threshold-good-prob}
 \mathbb P(\mathcal T^c)\leq\frac{4ph}{\rho^5}=O(\rho^2).$$ The Fubini calculation includes atoms; no regularity of the distribution of the minimizing measure is imposed here.

There will be a bank $\mathcal B_j$ at every level. Its size must compensate for the number of earlier reference tuples that can define the selection cell. Using all preceding levels would give a recursion of growing depth $p$; Proposition <a href="#prop:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:coverage">18</a> does not control its constants uniformly in $n$. The reset confines this recursion to $B$ fixed steps. Bank sizes can then depend only on the position within a block, keeping the total number of samples at $\exp(O(L))$. Older retained references will still enter the crossing constraints below.

<a id="eq:dyn-cell"></a>
<a id="eq:dyn-bank-property"></a>
We require coverage for every possible earlier tuple in the current block. For entries $u_i\in\mathcal B_i$ from those earlier levels, define $$\label{eq:dyn-cell}
 S=\{z:|R(u_i,z)|\leq3t_0
                    \text{ for every index in this tuple}\}.$$ The empty tuple gives the whole space. We require $$\label{eq:dyn-bank-property}
 x_k\in S,\quad 0\leq k\leq m
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \text{some }w\in\mathcal B_j\cap S
          \text{ satisfies }|R(w,x_k)|\geq q_*.$$ Absolute values make this property insensitive to orientations.

Choose the bank sizes recursively over the fixed positions $s=1,\ldots,B$. Once the constants at smaller positions are fixed, set $$C_s=\sum_{u<s}(D_{3,u}+2),\qquad D_{1,s}=C_s+5.$$ Let $D_{3,s}$ be a constant supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:coverage">18</a> for $D_{1,s}$, and use $$K_s=\lceil\exp((D_{3,s}+1)L)\rceil$$ entries at position $s$. For large $n$, the number of earlier tuples in the block is at most $\exp(C_sL)$. Only $B$ fixed applications of coverage are needed, so their disorder events have an intersection $\mathcal D$ with $\mathbb P(\mathcal D)\to1$.

Fix a level at position $s$, a realization $J\in\mathcal D$, and its earlier bank lists. Each cell $S$ has a bad subset $$B_S=\{x\in S:\pi^J\{z\in S:|R(z,x)|\geq q_*\}<e^{-D_{3,s}L}\}$$ of Gibbs mass less than $e^{-D_{1,s}L}$. The path is independent of those lists given $J$, and each deterministic-index marginal is $\pi^J$. A union bound over tuples and deterministic indices therefore bounds the probability that any $x_k$ lies in its cell’s bad subset by $$(m+1)e^{(C_s-D_{1,s})L}=O(e^{-3L}).$$ After excluding these bad targets, condition also on the complete path. The bank at the present level is still fresh. Its probability of missing a qualifying neighborhood inside the cell is at most $$(1-e^{-D_{3,s}L})^{K_s}\leq e^{-e^L}.$$ Union over the same tuples and indices, and then over the $p=O(L)$ levels, shows that <a href="#eq:dyn-bank-property" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-bank-property">[eq:dyn-bank-property]</a> holds simultaneously with probability $1-o(1)$. The recursion has only $B$ steps, so the total number of entries across all $p$ banks is $\exp(O(L))$.

We also impose simultaneous avoidance of the exceptional triples in Lemma <a href="#lem:fixed-locking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fixed-locking">12</a>, for the two cutoff pairs $(q_*,b)$ and $(b,b')$, and in Lemma <a href="#lem:narrow-locking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:narrow-locking">19</a>. We do so for every ordered triple of signed entries from distinct banks, and for every ordered triple consisting of two signed entries from distinct banks and one path point $x_k$, $0\leq k\leq m$. For each deterministic choice, the conditional law given $J$ is $(\pi^J)^{\otimes3}$: bank independence, stationarity at that deterministic index, and spin-flip symmetry suffice. The sampled configurations themselves need not be different.

<a id="eq:dyn-good-event-prob"></a>
There are $\exp(O(L))$ such choices, including all orders and signs. The exceptional probability for each is bounded by a constant times $e^{-n^{9/10}}+e^{-n^{4/5}}$. A union bound thus makes all these locking assertions simultaneous with probability $1-o(1)$. Let $\mathcal G$ be the intersection of this event, the simultaneous bank property, and $\mathcal T$. We have proved $$\label{eq:dyn-good-event-prob}
 \mathbb P(\mathcal G^c)=o(1).$$ We have made all these estimates simultaneously before choosing references or crossing times. Later application at a backwards crossing therefore uses this deterministic-index event, not an assertion of stationarity at a random time.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Backwards searches and deletion of conflicts

Select $v_1$ from $\mathcal B_1$ as the first entry whose absolute overlap with $x_0$ is at least $q_*$, and orient it positively. If it exists, let $\tau_1$ be the first $k\leq m$ with $R(v_1,x_k)\leq r_1$. On success initialize the ordered retained list as $A_1=\{1\}$.

At a subsequent level $j$, assuming all earlier levels have succeeded, form the cell <a href="#eq:dyn-cell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-cell">[eq:dyn-cell]</a> using the selected references in earlier positions of the current block. Choose as $v_j$ the first entry of $\mathcal B_j$ lying in this cell and having absolute overlap at least $q_*$ with $x_{\tau_{j-1}}$. Orient it so that $R(v_j,x_{\tau_{j-1}})\geq q_*$. From $A_{j-1}$ delete the conflicts, namely those indices $i$ with <a id="eq:dyn-conflict"></a>$$\label{eq:dyn-conflict}
 |R(v_i,v_j)|>b.$$ The remaining old indices are called compatible. Search backwards from $\tau_{j-1}$, stopping at the first point encountered with overlap at most $r_j$ with $v_j$. Equivalently, if the set is nonempty, define <a id="eq:dyn-backward-time"></a>$$\label{eq:dyn-backward-time}
 \tau_j=\max\{0\leq k\leq\tau_{j-1}:R(v_j,x_k)\leq r_j\}.$$ Add $j$ to the compatible list to obtain $A_j$.

To use independence of each fresh threshold, define $v_j$ on failure realizations as well. A missing candidate or missing crossing ends the procedure. A candidate already selected before its crossing fails is kept as that level’s reference. At a level with no candidate, and at all levels after an earlier failure, use the first entry of the corresponding bank without sign reversal. These defaults serve the final conditional-probability calculation. Selection and orientation of $v_j$ use only thresholds earlier than $r_j$; its own threshold is used solely for the subsequent crossing. The first reference, when selected successfully, uses only $J,x_0$ and $\mathcal B_1$ as required by <a href="#eq:dyn-tv-bridge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-tv-bridge">[eq:dyn-tv-bridge]</a>.

<a id="eq:dyn-induction-path"></a>
<a id="eq:dyn-induction-references"></a>
We now work deterministically on $\mathcal G\cap\mathcal A$. For all sufficiently large $n$, we will show that every level succeeds, that reference $1$ survives, and that $$\begin{aligned}
 |R(v_i,x_{\tau_j})-r_i|&\leq6j\varepsilon
                  &&(i\in A_j),\label{eq:dyn-induction-path}\\
 |R(v_i,v_\ell)-r_i|&\leq6j\varepsilon
                  &&(i,\ell\in A_j,\ i<\ell).
                  \label{eq:dyn-induction-references}
\end{aligned}$$ The induction also keeps the current block complete: after level $j$, all references from that block’s first level through $j$ remain on the list. At the next reset, the preceding block is therefore intact. Older blocks may already have gaps, but the complete preceding block will prevent a new deletion from reaching them.

<figure id="fig:protected-block">

<figcaption>Why a reset preserves the first reference. The dots are schematic slots in the original level order; omitted entries are indicated by ellipsis, and crossed gray slots represent deletions at earlier resets. Immediately before a reset at level $j$, the preceding block ${j − B, …, j − 1}$ is complete. Conflicts form a suffix of the retained list of length $d ≤ H_{0} < B$, illustrated in red. The suffix therefore misses both the first reference of that complete block and all older retained references. Thus $v_{1}$ survives at the first reset, when it belongs to the preceding block, and at every later reset, as drawn. The new reference $v_{j}$ is then appended. Older blocks may contain holes and need not be complete. The diagram illustrates a nonempty conflict suffix; the proof also permits $d = 0$.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-protected-block"></a>

Visual witness description of PDF page 48 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):
