```text
Three column headers organize the diagram from left to right:
1. "Older blocks with earlier deletions"
2. "Immediately preceding block all $B$ references still retained"
3. "New level $j$"

In the "Before reset" row:
- Under "Older blocks", there is a solid blue dot labeled $v_1$, followed by blue dots, an ellipsis ($\dots$), and further blue dots.
- Under "Immediately preceding block", entries begin with a blue dot labeled $v_{j-B}$, followed by blue dots, an ellipsis ($\dots$), and several dots. A red horizontal bracket spans the final dots in this block, labeled "Conflict suffix $d \le H_0 < B$", with the last dot in the block labeled $v_{j-1}$.

In the "After reset" row:
- Under "Older blocks", the solid blue dot $v_1$, subsequent blue dots, ellipsis ($\dots$), and blue dots remain unchanged.
- Under "Immediately preceding block", the non-conflicting blue dots are retained. The slots corresponding to the red conflict suffix above are shown as crossed circle symbols ($\oslash$) indicating deletion, with a vertical dashed arrow pointing down from the conflict suffix to them.
- Under "New level $j$", a vertical arrow labeled "Select and append" points down to a newly appended solid blue dot labeled $v_j$.
```


Deletions occur only at the first level of a new block, and at most $H_0$ indices, forming a suffix of the old list, are deleted at each such level.

<a id="eq:dyn-scale-relations"></a>
At level $1$, coverage of the whole space supplies the reference. Sign passage forces the first crossing, where the one-step overlap bound puts the value in $[r_1-2/n,r_1]$. This starts the induction. In the following induction we use the scale relations $$\label{eq:dyn-scale-relations}
 \frac1n\ll\eta_n=\rho^{100}\ll\varepsilon=\rho^{20},\qquad
 p\varepsilon=O(\rho^{19})\ll h=\rho^8\ll\rho.$$

Suppose the assertions hold through level $j-1$. All earlier references in the current block are still retained, since there have been no deletions within that block after its first level. Their overlaps with $x_{\tau_{j-1}}$ have absolute value at most $2t_0+6p\varepsilon<3t_0$. Thus this target belongs to the required cell, and the new selection $w=v_j$ exists by <a href="#eq:dyn-bank-property" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-bank-property">[eq:dyn-bank-property]</a>.

<a id="eq:dyn-target-transfer"></a>
<a id="eq:dyn-reference-transfer"></a>
For each compatible old index $i$, the two small overlaps in the triple $(v_i,w,x_{\tau_{j-1}})$ have absolute value at most $b$, whereas $R(w,x_{\tau_{j-1}})\geq q_*$. Fixed-scale locking gives $$\label{eq:dyn-target-transfer}
 |R(v_i,w)-R(v_i,x_{\tau_{j-1}})|\leq2\eta_n.$$ In particular, $$\label{eq:dyn-reference-transfer}
 |R(v_i,w)-r_i|\leq6(j-1)\varepsilon+2\eta_n
 \qquad(i\text{ compatible}).$$

**Conflicts occupy a bounded final segment.** A conflicting old index $i$ cannot precede a compatible old index $\ell$. Indeed, by <a href="#eq:dyn-induction-references" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-induction-references">[eq:dyn-induction-references]</a> and <a href="#eq:dyn-reference-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-reference-transfer">[eq:dyn-reference-transfer]</a>, the magnitudes of $R(v_\ell,v_i)$ and $R(v_\ell,w)$ are within $6(j-1)\varepsilon$ and $6(j-1)\varepsilon+2\eta_n$ of $r_i$ and $r_\ell$, respectively. Both magnitudes are less than $b'$, by <a href="#eq:dyn-t0-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-t0-choice">[eq:dyn-t0-choice]</a>. Their difference in magnitude is at least $$3\rho-12p\varepsilon-2\eta_n>2\eta_n.$$ On the other hand $|R(v_i,w)|>b$. Reverse one of this pair if necessary to make their mutual overlap positive. The signed difference of the two small overlaps has absolute value at least their difference in magnitude. This contradicts fixed-scale locking with cutoff pair $(b,b')$, which holds for all signs on $\mathcal G$. The conflicts consequently form a suffix of the old retained list.

The number of conflicts is bounded by $H_0$. Indeed, the induction bounds all absolute overlaps between retained old references by $2t_0+6p\varepsilon<3t_0$. If $d\geq1$ of them conflict with $w$, normalize them to unit vectors and reverse each sign to give inner product greater than $b$ with $w/\sqrt n$. Denote these vectors by $u_1,\ldots,u_d$. Then $$d^2b^2<\left\langle\sum_{a=1}^d u_a,\frac{w}{\sqrt n}\right\rangle^2
 \leq\lVert \sum_{a=1}^d u_a\rVert^2
 \leq d+3t_0d(d-1).$$ Since $3t_0<b^2/2$, this implies $$d<\frac{1-3t_0}{b^2-3t_0}<\frac{2}{b^2}\leq H_0.$$ In particular $d\leq H_0$.

If $j$ is not the first level of its block, the most recent old index $j-1$ is compatible: the cell condition gives $|R(v_{j-1},w)|\leq3t_0<b$. A suffix of conflicts is therefore empty. At the first level of a new block, the additional invariant says that all $B$ entries selected in the preceding block are still present. Removing at most $H_0<B$ final indices cannot reach any older block, or the first index of the preceding block. In particular index $1$ survives, including at the first reset. Adding the new index starts the current block as a retained singleton; at a nonreset it extends the complete current block because there is no deletion. This establishes the additional invariant and the deletion claims before proving that the new crossing exists.

<a id="eq:dyn-search-large-overlap"></a>
**The backwards search preserves old constraints.** Follow the search for $\tau_j$, including the crossing step when there is one. The new reference satisfies throughout this search $$\label{eq:dyn-search-large-overlap}
 R(w,x_k)\geq r_j-2/n.$$ If there is no crossing, this follows with the stronger strict bound $R(w,x_k)>r_j$ at every index down to zero. If there is a crossing, all indices strictly after it have overlap greater than $r_j$, and the jump bound gives <a href="#eq:dyn-search-large-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-search-large-overlap">[eq:dyn-search-large-overlap]</a> at the crossing itself.

<a id="eq:dyn-search-locking"></a>
For each compatible old reference $v_i$, <a href="#eq:dyn-reference-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-reference-transfer">[eq:dyn-reference-transfer]</a> places the fixed value $R(v_i,w)$ near $r_i$. The larger threshold $r_j$ separates the new overlap from that old level throughout the search. We will use narrow-annulus locking to prove $$\label{eq:dyn-search-locking}
 |R(v_i,x_k)-R(v_i,w)|<2\varepsilon$$ on this entire searched portion. Equation <a href="#eq:dyn-target-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-target-transfer">[eq:dyn-target-transfer]</a> proves the claim at the target. At a first violation while moving backwards, the one-step bound puts the left side in $[2\varepsilon,2\varepsilon+2/n]$. For this triple use the parameters of Lemma <a href="#lem:narrow-locking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:narrow-locking">19</a>: $$r=\frac{R(v_i,x_k)+R(v_i,w)}2,\qquad
 \delta=\frac{R(v_i,x_k)-R(v_i,w)}2,\qquad q=R(w,x_k).$$ Then $$\varepsilon\leq|\delta|\leq\varepsilon+1/n\leq2\varepsilon,
 \qquad
 |r-r_i|\leq6(j-1)\varepsilon+2\eta_n+\varepsilon+1/n<h.$$ It follows that $t_0/2<r<3t_0$ and $[r-h,r+h]\subseteq[r_i-2h,r_i+2h]$. Thus $\mathcal T$ gives the required bound $\mu([r-h,r+h])\leq\rho^4$. Also, by <a href="#eq:dyn-threshold-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-threshold-separation">[eq:dyn-threshold-separation]</a>, <a href="#eq:dyn-search-large-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-search-large-overlap">[eq:dyn-search-large-overlap]</a>, and <a href="#eq:dyn-scale-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-scale-relations">[eq:dyn-scale-relations]</a>, $$q-r\geq3\rho-6(j-1)\varepsilon-2\eta_n-\varepsilon-3/n
       \geq\rho.$$ All conditions of the forbidden narrow-annulus event hold, contradicting its simultaneous avoidance on $\mathcal G$. This proves <a href="#eq:dyn-search-locking" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-search-locking">[eq:dyn-search-locking]</a>.

The crossing must exist. Otherwise the search reaches index zero, and <a href="#eq:dyn-reference-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-reference-transfer">[eq:dyn-reference-transfer]</a>–<a href="#eq:dyn-search-locking" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-search-locking">[eq:dyn-search-locking]</a> apply there to the surviving compatible reference $1$. They would give $$R(v_1,x_0)<r_1+6(j-1)\varepsilon+2\eta_n+2\varepsilon<q_*,$$ contradicting the first selection. Hence the crossing exists. Its new reference overlap belongs to $[r_j-2/n,r_j]$. For each retained old reference, the error at the new crossing is at most $6(j-1)\varepsilon+2\eta_n+2\varepsilon\leq6j\varepsilon$. The old-new reference errors satisfy the same claimed bound by <a href="#eq:dyn-reference-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-reference-transfer">[eq:dyn-reference-transfer]</a>, and old-old reference errors are unchanged. This proves <a href="#eq:dyn-induction-path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-induction-path">[eq:dyn-induction-path]</a>–<a href="#eq:dyn-induction-references" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-induction-references">[eq:dyn-induction-references]</a> and completes the induction.

There are at most $p/B$ reset occasions, so the final list has size at least <a id="eq:dyn-retained-count"></a>$$\label{eq:dyn-retained-count}
 |A_p|\geq p-H_0p/B\geq p/B.$$ Consequently $\mathcal G\cap\mathcal A$ implies that, for some index $k$ in $\{0,\ldots,m\}$ and some subset of $p/B$ indices, the inequalities <a id="eq:dyn-small-target-windows"></a>$$\label{eq:dyn-small-target-windows}
 |R(v_i,x_k)-r_i|\leq6p\varepsilon$$ hold simultaneously. We have reduced sign passage to this existence event. The probability bound that follows will not condition on $\mathcal G$ or on the random retained list.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Predictable thresholds and completion of the proof

Let $\mathcal F_0$ be generated by $J$, all raw bank lists, and the path $(x_0,\ldots,x_m)$, and set $$\mathcal F_i=\sigma(\mathcal F_0,r_1,\ldots,r_i),
 \qquad 1\leq i\leq p.$$ Every $r_i$ is uniform on $I_i$ and independent of $\mathcal F_{i-1}$. The failure conventions ensure that each oriented $v_i$ is $\mathcal F_{i-1}$-measurable. Here is the complete induction. At level $1$ its selection does not use any threshold. Inductively, whether a prior level failed, the previously selected references, and the preceding crossing time are all determined by earlier thresholds. Selection at level $i$ uses only that information and the bank and path data in $\mathcal F_0$. The subsequent crossing may use $r_i$, but never changes that selected reference. A default caused by an earlier failure likewise depends only on earlier thresholds. This proves the claim, including on failure events.

<a id="eq:dyn-conditional-window"></a>
For each deterministic $k\leq m$, let $$E_i(k)=\{|R(v_i,x_k)-r_i|\leq6p\varepsilon\},\qquad
 \gamma_n=\frac{12p\varepsilon}{\rho}.$$ The interval defining $E_i(k)$ has length at most $12p\varepsilon$, while $I_i$ has length $\rho$. Thus, for all sufficiently large $n$, $$\label{eq:dyn-conditional-window}
 \mathbb P(E_i(k)\mid\mathcal F_{i-1})\leq\gamma_n<1.$$ For a deterministic subset $\{i_1<\cdots<i_s\}$, the earlier events $E_{i_1}(k),\ldots,E_{i_{s-1}}(k)$ are $\mathcal F_{i_s-1}$-measurable. Conditioning first at the largest selected index and using <a href="#eq:dyn-conditional-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-conditional-window">[eq:dyn-conditional-window]</a> yields $$\mathbb P\left(\bigcap_{a=1}^s E_{i_a}(k)\right)
 \leq\gamma_n\,
 \mathbb P\left(\bigcap_{a=1}^{s-1}E_{i_a}(k)\right)
 \leq\gamma_n^s.$$ The elimination order is essential: the latest selected threshold is integrated out first. Later centers may depend on earlier thresholds, but the displayed conditioning remains valid. Neither the random retained subset nor the good event has been conditioned on.

Taking $s=p/B$, then taking a union over all deterministic subsets and all deterministic indices $k$, proves <a id="eq:dyn-threshold-union"></a>$$\label{eq:dyn-threshold-union}
 \mathbb P\{\text{\eqref{eq:dyn-small-target-windows} holds for some }k
                  \text{ and some subset of size }p/B\}
 \leq(m+1)2^p\left(\frac{12p\varepsilon}{\rho}\right)^{p/B}
 =o(1).$$ Indeed $p=(t_0/5)L+O(1)$, $\rho=L^{-1}$, and $12p\varepsilon/\rho=O(\rho^{18})$. The logarithm of the right-hand side is therefore bounded above by $$2L+O(p)-\frac{18p}{B}\log(1/\rho)
       =-\Omega(L\log L).$$

The deterministic induction and <a href="#eq:dyn-good-event-prob" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-good-event-prob">[eq:dyn-good-event-prob]</a> now imply $$\mathbb P(\mathcal A)
 \leq\mathbb P(\mathcal G^c)
       +\mathbb P(\mathcal G\cap\mathcal A)=o(1).$$ The first selection succeeds on the simultaneous bank event at level $1$, so $\mathbb P(\mathcal S^c)=o(1)$ as well. Finally, <a href="#eq:dyn-tv-bridge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-tv-bridge">[eq:dyn-tv-bridge]</a> and <a href="#eq:dyn-poisson-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-poisson-tail">[eq:dyn-poisson-tail]</a> give $$\mathbb P(\mathcal M)
 \leq\mathbb P(\mathcal S^c)+4\mathbb P(\mathcal A)
       +4\mathbb P\{N(e^L)>m\}=o(1).$$ This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> also under <a href="#eq:dyn-identity-interval" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyn-identity-interval">[eq:dyn-identity-interval]</a>, and the two cases exhaust the possibilities.

Finally take $T_n=\lfloor e^L\rfloor$ discrete update attempts. This deterministic index lies below $m$ for large $n$. If its transition law is within $1/4$ of equilibrium, the opposite-sign half-space has conditional probability at least $1/4$ at that index. Thus the same sign-passage inequality holds with no clock-overrun term. All bank, locking and threshold estimates already concern the discrete path, so they prove the second assertion of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> at exactly $\lfloor e^L\rfloor$ attempts.

<a id="poly-sec:coverage"></a>
### Coverage inside sets of polynomial Gibbs mass

The fixed-depth crossing argument in Appendix <a href="#fixed-sec:crossings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fixed-sec:crossings">10</a> uses polynomially many reference samples. The neighborhood bound $e^{-D_3n^\kappa}$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:coverage">18</a> is smaller than every fixed inverse power of $n$, so it does not supply those references. We therefore apply the same energy and rotation mechanism with a perturbation variance of order $\log n/n$. This yields polynomial overlap mass inside sets of polynomial Gibbs mass, uniformly over every subset of the cube. We give the argument from the energy and rotation estimates in Section <a href="#sec:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:energy">5</a>, so that the fixed-depth route can be read independently of the growing-depth construction.

Throughout this appendix, fix $\beta>1$ and use the off-diagonal Hamiltonian of Equation <a href="#eq:model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:model">[eq:model]</a>. Write $H^J=H_n^J$, $Z^J=Z_{n,\beta}^J$, and $\pi^J=\pi_{n,\beta}^J$. For a nonempty $S\subset\{-1,1\}^n$, define its conditional Gibbs measure by $$\pi_S^J(x)=\frac{\pi^J(x)\mathbf1_{\{x\in S\}}}{\pi^J(S)}.$$ Every nonempty set has positive Gibbs mass. All Gaussian disorder vectors below have independent standard normal coordinates indexed by $i<j$.

<a id="stmt-poly-prop-coverage"></a>
<div id="poly-prop:coverage" class="proposition">

<a id="poly-eq:coverage-uniform"></a>
**Proposition 20** (Polynomial overlap coverage). *There exists $q_*=q_*(\beta)\in(0,1)$ such that, for every fixed $D_1>0$, there exists a fixed $D_2>0$ for which $$\label{poly-eq:coverage-uniform}
 \mathbb P_J\!\left(
  \text{for every }S\subset\{-1,1\}^n\text{ with }\pi^J(S)\ge n^{-D_1},\quad
  (\pi_S^J)^{\otimes2}\{|R|\ge q_*\}\ge n^{-D_2}
 \right)\longrightarrow1.$$ The threshold $q_*$ does not depend on $D_1$.*

</div>

The proof compares two estimates for the change in $\log Z$ under a small Gaussian rotation. If a set has too few overlapping pairs, a fresh Gaussian perturbation increases its restricted partition sum. The energy upper tail limits the loss caused by decreasing the coefficient of the original disorder. Together these give an increase that the rotation estimate makes unlikely. We first prove the required gain for an arbitrary deterministic probability measure, so that it can later be applied after conditioning on $J$.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Gain under independent Gaussian disorder

<a id="stmt-poly-lem-coverage-gain"></a>
<div id="poly-lem:coverage-gain" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (Gaussian gain from few overlapping pairs). *Fix $M>0$, $q\in(0,1)$, and $D\ge\beta^2M/2+2$. For all sufficiently large $n$, set $t=M\log n/n$. If $\nu$ is any deterministic probability measure on $\{-1,1\}^n$ satisfying $$\nu^{\otimes2}\{|R|\ge q\}\le4n^{-D},$$ and $G$ is standard Gaussian, define $W=\nu(e^{\beta\sqrt t H^G})$. There is a constant $C_\beta$, independent of $M,q,D,n,\nu$, such that <a id="poly-eq:coverage-gain"></a>$$\label{poly-eq:coverage-gain}
 \mathbb P_G\!\left(
 \log W\ge\frac{\beta^2t(n-1)}4
       -\beta^2ntq-C_\beta\sqrt{nt}-\log2
 \right)\ge\frac12.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-poly-lem-coverage-gain"><summary>Proof of stmt-poly-lem-coverage-gain</summary>

<a id="poly-eq:coverage-pz"></a>
*Proof.* For $x\in\{-1,1\}^n$ let $h_x=(n^{-1/2}x_ix_j)_{i<j}$, so that $H^G(x)=h_x\cdot G$. In particular, $$|h_x|^2=\frac{n-1}{2},\qquad
 \mathbb E_G[H^G(x)H^G(y)]=\frac{nR(x,y)^2-1}{2}.$$ Set $$m_0=\frac{\beta^2t(n-1)}4,\qquad
 A=\frac{\beta^2ntq^2}{2}.$$ The Gaussian exponential moment formula gives $\mathbb E_GW=e^{m_0}$. For the second moment, split pairs according to whether $|R|<q$: <a id="poly-eq:coverage-moments"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{\mathbb E_GW^2}{(\mathbb E_GW)^2}
 &=\nu^{\otimes2}\!\left(e^{\beta^2t(nR^2-1)/2}\right)\notag\\
 &\le e^A+4n^{-D}e^{\beta^2nt/2}
 \le e^A+4n^{-2}
 \le2e^A.
 \label{poly-eq:coverage-moments}
\end{aligned}$$ The exponent assumption on $D$ gives the second inequality, and the last one holds for $n\ge2$. Paley–Zygmund therefore implies $$\label{poly-eq:coverage-pz}
 \mathbb P_G(\log W\ge m_0-\log2)\ge\frac18e^{-A}.$$ This probability may be small. Gaussian concentration will turn it into a lower bound of probability at least one half, while controlling the loss in the value of $\log W$.

<a id="poly-eq:coverage-mean-gain"></a>
Write $Y(G)=\log W$. Differentiation expresses its gradient as $\beta\sqrt t$ times a convex combination of the vectors $h_x$. Consequently $Y$ is Lipschitz with constant at most $$L_W=\beta\sqrt{\frac{t(n-1)}2}.$$ The Gaussian moment bound in Equation <a href="#static-eq:gaussian-mgf" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="static-eq:gaussian-mgf">[static-eq:gaussian-mgf]</a> of Lemma <a href="#static-lem:gaussian-tools" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:gaussian-tools">3</a>, followed by Chernoff’s inequality, gives the following one-sided tails for this deterministic function of $G$: $$\mathbb P_G(Y-\mathbb E_GY\ge s)\le e^{-s^2/(2L_W^2)},\qquad
 \mathbb P_G(Y-\mathbb E_GY\le-s)\le e^{-s^2/(2L_W^2)}
 \quad(s>0).$$ If $m_0-\log2>\mathbb E_GY$, comparison of the upper-tail bound with Equation <a href="#poly-eq:coverage-pz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly-eq:coverage-pz">[poly-eq:coverage-pz]</a> gives $$\mathbb E_GY\ge m_0-\log2-L_W\sqrt{2(A+\log8)}.$$ If $m_0-\log2\le\mathbb E_GY$, the same inequality holds immediately. Using $\sqrt{a+b}\le\sqrt a+\sqrt b$, we thus obtain $$\label{poly-eq:coverage-mean-gain}
 \mathbb E_GY\ge m_0-\log2
        -\frac{\beta^2ntq}{\sqrt2}
        -\beta\sqrt{nt\log8}.$$ The lower-tail bound also gives $Y\ge\mathbb E_GY-L_W\sqrt{2\log2}$ with probability at least $1/2$. Combining this with Equation <a href="#poly-eq:coverage-mean-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly-eq:coverage-mean-gain">[poly-eq:coverage-mean-gain]</a>, and bounding $\beta^2ntq/\sqrt2$ by $\beta^2ntq$, proves the stated estimate. One admissible choice is $$C_\beta=\beta\bigl(\sqrt{\log8}+\sqrt{\log2}\bigr).$$ This constant is independent of the measure $\nu$ and of the parameters $M,q,D$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Uniform coverage over all subsets

<details id="proof-stmt-poly-lem-coverage-gain-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-poly-lem-coverage-gain</summary>

<a id="poly-eq:energy-event"></a>
<a id="poly-eq:coverage-constants"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#poly-prop:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly-prop:coverage">20</a>.* Let $e_1\in(0,\beta/2)$ and $c_E>0$ be the constants from Lemma <a href="#static-lem:coverage-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:coverage-energy">16</a>. Thus, outside a disorder event of probability at most $e^{-c_En}$, $$\label{poly-eq:energy-event}
 \pi^J\{H^J>ne_1\}\le e^{-c_En}.$$ Define $$g=\frac{\beta^2}{4}-\frac{\beta e_1}{2}>0.$$ Choose the overlap threshold before choosing any mass exponent: $$\label{poly-eq:coverage-constants}
 0<q_*<\min\left\{1,\frac{g}{8\beta^2}\right\}.$$ It depends only on $\beta$. We choose this same threshold in Proposition <a href="#prop:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:coverage">18</a>: both coverage arguments use the same $e_1$ and permit the strict bound in Equation <a href="#poly-eq:coverage-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly-eq:coverage-constants">[poly-eq:coverage-constants]</a>. Now fix $D_1>0$ and choose <a id="poly-eq:coverage-parameters"></a>$$\label{poly-eq:coverage-parameters}
 M\ge\frac{8(D_1+1)}g,\qquad
 D_2=\frac{\beta^2M}{2}+2,\qquad
 t=\frac{M\log n}{n},\qquad \theta=\arcsin\sqrt t.$$ For all sufficiently large $n$, $0<t<1/2$. All constants have been fixed independently of $n$ and of the disorder.

<a id="poly-eq:violating-set"></a>
Let $\mathcal B_n$ be the event that Equation <a href="#poly-eq:energy-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly-eq:energy-event">[poly-eq:energy-event]</a> holds and that some $S\subset\{-1,1\}^n$ satisfies $$\label{poly-eq:violating-set}
 \pi^J(S)\ge n^{-D_1},\qquad
 (\pi_S^J)^{\otimes2}\{|R|\ge q_*\}<n^{-D_2}.$$ We will bound the probability of this existence event by selecting one violating set. Fix a deterministic ordering of the subsets of the finite cube and take its first member satisfying Equation <a href="#poly-eq:violating-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly-eq:violating-set">[poly-eq:violating-set]</a>. For each fixed nonempty set, both Gibbs quantities in that equation are continuous functions of $J$. The resulting selector $S=S(J)$ is therefore measurable on $\mathcal B_n$. In particular, it is fixed when we condition on $J$.

For such a fixed $J$, remove the part of $S$ above the energy threshold: $$T=S\cap\{x:H^J(x)\le ne_1\},\qquad \nu=\pi_T^J.$$ For all sufficiently large $n$, uniformly over $J\in\mathcal B_n$, <a id="poly-eq:trimmed-mass"></a>$$\label{poly-eq:trimmed-mass}
 \pi^J(T)\ge\pi^J(S)-e^{-c_En}
       \ge\frac12\pi^J(S)\ge\frac12n^{-D_1}.$$ In particular, $T$ is nonempty. Since $T\subset S$, the two conditioning denominators give <a id="poly-eq:trimmed-pairs"></a>$$\label{poly-eq:trimmed-pairs}
 \nu^{\otimes2}\{|R|\ge q_*\}
 \le\left(\frac{\pi^J(S)}{\pi^J(T)}\right)^2
       (\pi_S^J)^{\otimes2}\{|R|\ge q_*\}
 <4n^{-D_2}.$$ The trimmed measure is measurable in $J$ as well. Draw $G$ independently of $J$. Conditional on $J$, the measure $\nu$ is deterministic, so Lemma <a href="#poly-lem:coverage-gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly-lem:coverage-gain">21</a> applies with $q=q_*$ and $D=D_2$. Write $$W=\nu(e^{\beta\sqrt t H^G}),\qquad
 J_\theta=\sqrt{1-t}\,J+\sqrt t\,G.$$ No part of the choice of $S,T$, or $\nu$ uses this fresh disorder.

<a id="poly-eq:partition-gain"></a>
<a id="poly-eq:bad-increment"></a>
We now compare this conditional gain with the full partition sum. Linearity in the disorder, restriction to $T$, and $\sqrt{1-t}-1<0$ imply $$\begin{aligned}
 \log\frac{Z^{J_\theta}}{Z^J}
 &=\log\pi^J\!\left(
      e^{\beta(\sqrt{1-t}-1)H^J+\beta\sqrt t H^G}\right)\\
 &\ge-D_1\log n-\log2
       -\beta(1-\sqrt{1-t})ne_1+\log W.
\end{aligned}$$ Thus Lemma <a href="#poly-lem:coverage-gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly-lem:coverage-gain">21</a> gives, with conditional probability at least $1/2$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \log\frac{Z^{J_\theta}}{Z^J}
 &\ge-D_1\log n-2\log2
       -\beta(1-\sqrt{1-t})ne_1
       +\frac{\beta^2t(n-1)}4
       -\beta^2ntq_*-C_\beta\sqrt{nt}\notag\\
 &\ge nt\left(g-\beta^2q_*-\frac{D_1}{M}
                         -\frac{\beta e_1t}{2}\right)
       -C_\beta\sqrt{nt}-2\log2-\frac{\beta^2t}{4}.
 \label{poly-eq:partition-gain}
\end{aligned}$$ The last line uses $1-\sqrt{1-t}\le t/2+t^2/2$ for $0\le t<1$. Our choices ensure $\beta^2q_*<g/8$ and $D_1/M<g/8$. Since $t\to0$ and $nt=M\log n\to\infty$, the remaining errors in Equation <a href="#poly-eq:partition-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly-eq:partition-gain">[poly-eq:partition-gain]</a> are $o(nt)$. Consequently, for all sufficiently large $n$ and every $J\in\mathcal B_n$, $$\label{poly-eq:bad-increment}
 \mathbb P_G\!\left(
   \log Z^{J_\theta}-\log Z^J\ge\frac g2nt\,\middle|\,J
 \right)\ge\frac12.$$ The lower bound on $n$ depends only on the fixed parameters and is uniform in $J$ and its selected set.

It remains to bound this increment under the joint law of $(J,G)$. For $t\le1/2$, $\theta\le\sqrt{2t}$. The unconditional rotation estimate in Lemma <a href="#static-lem:coverage-rotation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:coverage-rotation">17</a> therefore yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P_{J,G}\!\left(
   \log Z^{J_\theta}-\log Z^J\ge\frac g2nt\right)
 &\le\exp\!\left(-\frac{g^2nt}{8\beta^2}\right)\\
 &=n^{-g^2M/(8\beta^2)}.
\end{aligned}$$ Integrating Equation <a href="#poly-eq:bad-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly-eq:bad-increment">[poly-eq:bad-increment]</a> over $\mathcal B_n$ bounds $\mathbb P_J(\mathcal B_n)$ by twice this probability. Adding the exceptional event from Lemma <a href="#static-lem:coverage-energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-lem:coverage-energy">16</a>, we conclude that the failure probability in Equation <a href="#poly-eq:coverage-uniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly-eq:coverage-uniform">[poly-eq:coverage-uniform]</a> is at most <a id="poly-eq:failure-bound"></a>$$\label{poly-eq:failure-bound}
 e^{-c_En}+2n^{-g^2M/(8\beta^2)}\longrightarrow0.$$ The selector controls the existence of any violating subset, without a union bound over subsets of the cube. The choice of $q_*$ in Equation <a href="#poly-eq:coverage-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly-eq:coverage-constants">[poly-eq:coverage-constants]</a> preceded the choice of $D_1$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Target coverage within arbitrary subsets

For the crossing argument, we need a bound on the total Gibbs mass of targets lacking polynomial Gibbs mass of overlapping references in the same subset. Both masses below use the original Gibbs measure. This is the form needed when targets have Gibbs marginals and reference banks contain unconditioned Gibbs samples. Uniformity of the pair statement gives the target bound by applying it to the set of points that would violate it.

<a id="stmt-poly-cor-coverage-cells"></a>
<div id="poly-cor:coverage-cells" class="corollary">

<a id="poly-eq:coverage-cells"></a>
**Corollary 22** (Target coverage within arbitrary subsets). *Let $q_*$ be as in Proposition <a href="#poly-prop:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly-prop:coverage">20</a>. For every fixed $D_1>0$, there exists a fixed $D_3>0$ such that, with probability tending to one over $J$, every $S\subset\{-1,1\}^n$ satisfies $$\label{poly-eq:coverage-cells}
 \pi^J\!\left(
  \left\{x\in S:
   \pi^J\{b\in S:|R(x,b)|\ge q_*\}<n^{-D_3}
  \right\}\right)<n^{-D_1}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-poly-cor-coverage-cells"><summary>Proof of stmt-poly-cor-coverage-cells</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $D_1>0$, take the corresponding $D_2$ from Proposition <a href="#poly-prop:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly-prop:coverage">20</a>, and choose any fixed $D_3>D_1+D_2$. Work on the simultaneous event in that proposition. Given $S\subset\{-1,1\}^n$, denote the set inside the outer measure in Equation <a href="#poly-eq:coverage-cells" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="poly-eq:coverage-cells">[poly-eq:coverage-cells]</a> by $B$. If $m=\pi^J(B)\ge n^{-D_1}$, then $B$ is nonempty, and $B\subset S$ implies $$\begin{aligned}
 (\pi_B^J)^{\otimes2}\{|R|\ge q_*\}
 &=\frac1{m^2}\sum_{x\in B}\pi^J(x)
             \pi^J\{b\in B:|R(x,b)|\ge q_*\}\\
 &<\frac{n^{-D_3}}m
 \le n^{-D_3+D_1}<n^{-D_2}.
\end{aligned}$$ This contradicts Proposition <a href="#poly-prop:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly-prop:coverage">20</a> applied to $B$. Hence $\pi^J(B)<n^{-D_1}$. This implication is deterministic on the same disorder event for every $S$, including cells selected after observing $J$. For $S=\varnothing$ the assertion is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="fixed-sec:crossings"></a>
### A fixed number of crossings on polynomial time scales

The polynomial coverage estimate in Proposition <a href="#poly-prop:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly-prop:coverage">20</a> has a direct dynamical application. Together with the absolute-overlap locking estimate of Proposition <a href="#static-prop:locking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-prop:locking">15</a>, it rules out mixing on each fixed polynomial time scale by using a fixed number of nested crossings. We give the argument in full. Its number of levels and threshold intervals remain fixed as $n$ grows.

<a id="stmt-fixed-thm-polynomial"></a>
<div id="fixed-thm:polynomial" class="theorem">

<a id="fixed-eq:continuous-conclusion"></a>
<a id="fixed-eq:discrete-conclusion"></a>
**Theorem 23** (Polynomial-time obstruction from fixed-depth crossings). *For every fixed $\beta>1$ and every fixed $a>0$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P_{J,\,x_0\sim\pi^J}
 \left\{d^J(x_0,n^a)>\frac14\right\}&\longrightarrow1,
 \label{fixed-eq:continuous-conclusion}\\
 \mathbb P_{J,\,x_0\sim\pi^J}
 \left\{d_{\mathrm{disc}}^J(x_0,\lfloor n^a\rfloor)
                                      >\frac14\right\}
 &\longrightarrow1.
 \label{fixed-eq:discrete-conclusion}
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently both $\mathbb P\{T^J(x_0)>n^a\}$ and $\mathbb P\{T_{\mathrm{disc}}^J(x_0)>n^a\}$ tend to one under the same joint law. Each assertion is equivalently a statement that the Gibbs mass of the indicated initial states tends to one in probability over $J$.*

</div>

As in the main theorem, the transition law in each distance starts from the realized state $x_0$. The proof uses stationarity to sample possible targets along a path, and then uses total variation from that fixed initial state to force a sign passage. Independent random thresholds will make such a passage unlikely.

<a id="fixed-subsec:path-scales"></a>
#### A stationary path and fixed overlap scales

<a id="fixed-eq:stationary-marginal"></a>
<a id="fixed-eq:poisson-tail"></a>
Fix $\beta>1$ and $a>0$, and abbreviate $\pi^J$ to $\pi$. Put <a id="fixed-eq:time-parameters"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:time-parameters}
 t_n=n^a,\qquad m=\lceil2n^{a+1}\rceil,\qquad
 e_*=\frac1{100},\qquad \delta_n=n^{-e_*}.$$ Conditional on $J$, start $x_0$ with law $\pi$ and generate an infinite discrete heat-bath chain $(x_k)_{k\ge0}$. At each step choose a uniform site and use fresh resampling randomness, including possible self-transitions. Let $N(t)$ be an independent rate-$n$ Poisson process. The process $x_{N(t)}$ is the continuous-time chain with rate one at each site. Indeed, if $K_i^J$ denotes resampling at site $i$, then $K^J=n^{-1}\sum_iK_i^J$ and its generator is $\sum_i(K_i^J-I)=n(K^J-I)$. Therefore $$\mathbb E[(K^J)^{N(t)}]=\exp\{nt(K^J-I)\}=P_t^J.$$ Stationarity gives, for every deterministic $k$, $$\label{fixed-eq:stationary-marginal}
 \mathcal L(x_k\mid J)=\pi.$$ Moreover, Chernoff’s inequality at parameter $\log2$, with $\mathbb EN(t_n)=n^{a+1}$ and $m\ge2n^{a+1}$, gives $$\label{fixed-eq:poisson-tail}
 \eta_n:=\mathbb P\{N(t_n)>m\}
 \le\exp\{-(2\log2-1)n^{a+1}\}=o(1).$$

<a id="fixed-eq:depth-count"></a>
Use the common threshold $q_*=q_*(\beta)>0$ of Proposition <a href="#poly-prop:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly-prop:coverage">20</a>. With the same energy cutoff $e_1$ as in the main coverage argument, its choice satisfies $$g=\frac{\beta^2}{4}-\frac{\beta e_1}{2}>0,\qquad
 0<q_*<\min\{1,g/(8\beta^2)\}.$$ Choose an integer $p\ge2$ such that $$\label{fixed-eq:depth-count}
 pe_*>a+2.$$ This integer is fixed throughout the limit. First choose $t_p>0$ with $2t_p<q_*/2$. For $j=p,p-1,\ldots,2$, take a cutoff $b_j\in(0,q_*/2)$ allowed by Proposition <a href="#static-prop:locking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-prop:locking">15</a> for large-overlap threshold $h=t_j/2$, and then choose $t_{j-1}>0$ with <a id="fixed-eq:separated-scales"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:separated-scales}
 t_{j-1}<t_j/10,\qquad 4t_{j-1}<b_j.$$ Thus $0<t_1<\cdots<t_p$, and all scale gaps are fixed positive constants. Independently of the disorder and of all other randomness introduced below, draw <a id="fixed-eq:thresholds"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:thresholds}
 r_j\sim\operatorname{Unif}[t_j,2t_j],\qquad 1\le j\le p,$$ independently for different $j$.

<a id="fixed-subsec:banks"></a>
#### Polynomial reference banks cover every possible target

We next sample finite lists of Gibbs configurations. These lists provide references for the later crossing construction. Their sizes are chosen so that coverage holds for every earlier reference choice and every deterministic index of the path segment $x_0,\ldots,x_m$.

Conditional on $J$, all entries in all banks are independent with law $\pi$, and all banks are independent of the entire chain. Choose their sizes recursively. Having chosen $D_{3,i}$ for $i<j$, set $$B_j=\sum_{i<j}(D_{3,i}+1),\qquad B_1=0,$$ choose $D_{1,j}>B_j+a+3$, and use Corollary <a href="#poly-cor:coverage-cells" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly-cor:coverage-cells">22</a> with exponent $D_{1,j}$ to obtain $D_{3,j}>0$. Bank $j$ consists of <a id="fixed-eq:bank-sizes"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:bank-sizes}
 M_j=\lceil n^{D_{3,j}+1}\rceil
 \quad\hbox{entries}\quad V_{j,1},\ldots,V_{j,M_j}.$$ The constants are chosen before any bank entries or thresholds are sampled. In particular, the number of tuples with one entry from each of the first $j-1$ banks satisfies <a id="fixed-eq:tuple-count"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:tuple-count}
 Q_j:=\prod_{i<j}M_i\le2^{j-1}n^{B_j}.$$ For the empty product, $Q_1=1$.

<a id="fixed-eq:cell"></a>
For such a tuple $\boldsymbol v=(v_1,\ldots,v_{j-1})$, define its cell by $$\label{fixed-eq:cell}
 S_j(\boldsymbol v)
 =\{z\in\{-1,1\}^n: |R(v_i,z)|\le3t_i\text{ for every }i<j\}.$$ In particular $S_1=\{-1,1\}^n$. The cell restricts the small overlaps with the earlier references, while coverage will supply a large overlap with the current target inside that cell.

<a id="stmt-fixed-lem-bank-coverage"></a>
<div id="fixed-lem:bank-coverage" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Simultaneous bank coverage). *With probability $1-o(1)$, for every $1\le j\le p$, every tuple $\boldsymbol v$ from banks $1,\ldots,j-1$, and every $0\le k\le m$, $$x_k\in S_j(\boldsymbol v)
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \text{there is }\ell\le M_j\text{ such that }
 V_{j,\ell}\in S_j(\boldsymbol v),\quad
 |R(V_{j,\ell},x_k)|\ge q_*.$$ This event depends only on $J$, the chain and the bank lists, and is independent of the thresholds.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fixed-lem-bank-coverage"><summary>Proof of stmt-fixed-lem-bank-coverage</summary>

*Proof.* Intersect the $p$ disorder events from Corollary <a href="#poly-cor:coverage-cells" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="poly-cor:coverage-cells">22</a>. Since $p$ is fixed, the intersection has probability $1-o(1)$. On it, for every $S\subseteq\{-1,1\}^n$ the set $$\mathcal B_j(S)=
 \{x\in S: \pi\{z\in S:|R(z,x)|\ge q_*\}<n^{-D_{3,j}}\}$$ has $\pi$-mass less than $n^{-D_{1,j}}$.

Fix a disorder in this intersection and condition on the complete first $j-1$ banks. Each cell in <a href="#fixed-eq:cell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:cell">[fixed-eq:cell]</a> is then fixed. The path remains independent of these banks conditional on $J$, so <a href="#fixed-eq:stationary-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:stationary-marginal">[fixed-eq:stationary-marginal]</a> applies to every deterministic $k$. A union bound over all tuples and all indices gives <a id="fixed-eq:bad-target-bound"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:bad-target-bound}
 \mathbb P\!\left\{
 x_k\in\mathcal B_j(S_j(\boldsymbol v))
 \text{ for some }\boldsymbol v,k
 \,\middle|\, J,\text{ banks }1,\ldots,j-1\right\}
 \le Q_j(m+1)n^{-D_{1,j}}.$$ This bound uses marginal stationarity, without requiring independence between different times.

Now condition also on the entire chain. For a target $x_k$ in its cell but outside its bad set, acceptable references have $\pi$-mass at least $n^{-D_{3,j}}$. The entries of bank $j$ are still independent Gibbs draws under this conditioning. The chance that this bank misses that set is at most $$(1-n^{-D_{3,j}})^{M_j}
 \le\exp(-M_jn^{-D_{3,j}})\le e^{-n}.$$ Another union bound gives $Q_j(m+1)e^{-n}$. Combining the two bounds and summing over the fixed number of banks, the total failure probability is at most <a id="fixed-eq:coverage-failure"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:coverage-failure}
 o(1)+\sum_{j=1}^pQ_j(m+1)
              (n^{-D_{1,j}}+e^{-n})=o(1).$$ Indeed $Q_j(m+1)=O(n^{B_j+a+1})$ and $D_{1,j}>B_j+a+3$. Neither part of the event uses any threshold. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-fixed-lem-bank-locking"></a>
<div id="fixed-lem:bank-locking" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Simultaneous bank locking). *With probability $1-o(1)$, for every $i<j\le p$, every entry $v$ of bank $i$, every entry $w$ of bank $j$, and every $0\le k\le m$, <a id="fixed-eq:all-bank-locking"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:all-bank-locking}
 \begin{gathered}
 |R(x_k,w)|\ge t_j/2,\qquad
 |R(v,x_k)|,\ |R(v,w)|\le b_j
 \\
 \Longrightarrow\qquad
 \bigl||R(v,x_k)|-|R(v,w)|\bigr|\le2\delta_n.
 \end{gathered}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fixed-lem-bank-locking"><summary>Proof of stmt-fixed-lem-bank-locking</summary>

*Proof.* For fixed bank indices and deterministic $k$, the triple $(v,x_k,w)$ has law $\pi^{\otimes3}$ conditional on $J$. This follows from the independence of the two distinct banks and their independence from the stationary chain. Proposition <a href="#static-prop:locking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="static-prop:locking">15</a>, with $h=t_j/2$ and cutoff $b_j$, bounds the averaged failure probability by $e^{-n^{9/10}}$. There are only finitely many choices of $j$ for the large-$n$ threshold in that proposition. A union bound therefore gives the failure estimate <a id="fixed-eq:locking-failure"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:locking-failure}
 \sum_{i<j}(m+1)M_iM_j e^{-n^{9/10}}=o(1).$$ Every bank exponent and $p$ are fixed, so the prefactor is polynomial. ◻

</details>

<a id="fixed-eq:good-event"></a>
Let $\mathcal G_n$ be the intersection of the events in the two lemmas. Then $$\label{fixed-eq:good-event}
 \mathbb P(\mathcal G_n^c)=o(1).$$ It is determined by $J,x_0,\ldots,x_m$ and the bank lists. We have now obtained coverage and locking simultaneously for every choice the crossing construction can make; the thresholds remain independent of this event.

<a id="fixed-subsec:construction"></a>
#### A measurable construction of nested crossings

<a id="fixed-eq:one-step"></a>
For a reference $v\in\{-1,1\}^n$, write $D_v(k)=|R(v,x_k)|$. A single update changes at most one spin, so $$\label{fixed-eq:one-step}
 |D_v(k+1)-D_v(k)|\le2/n
 \qquad\text{for every }v,k.$$ We select the first acceptable entry in each bank, in its given order. To keep every selected reference defined even on exceptional events, we specify both successful selections and defaults.

<a id="fixed-eq:first-crossing"></a>
At level $1$, choose $v_1$ as the first entry of bank $1$ satisfying $D_{v_1}(0)\ge q_*$. If there is no such entry, set $v_1=V_{1,1}$, $\tau_1=0$, and declare the construction inactive. If selection succeeds, set $$\label{fixed-eq:first-crossing}
 \tau_1=\min\{0\le k\le m:D_{v_1}(k)\le r_1\}$$ when the set is nonempty, and declare the construction active. If that set is empty, set $\tau_1=0$ and declare it inactive.

Suppose $j\ge2$ and the earlier levels are defined. If the construction is active and $x_{\tau_{j-1}}\in S_j(v_1,\ldots,v_{j-1})$, select $v_j$ as the first entry of bank $j$ in that cell with $$|R(v_j,x_{\tau_{j-1}})|\ge q_*.$$ After a successful selection, search backwards from $\tau_{j-1}$: <a id="fixed-eq:backward-crossing"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:backward-crossing}
 \tau_j=\max\{0\le k\le\tau_{j-1}:D_{v_j}(k)\le r_j\}$$ if this set is nonempty, keeping the construction active. If it is empty, retain the selected $v_j$, set $\tau_j=0$, and declare the construction inactive. If the construction was already inactive, the target was outside its cell, or the reference selection failed, set $v_j=V_{j,1}$ and $\tau_j=0$, and declare it inactive. An inactive construction stays inactive at all later levels. At every successful selection, the depth at the target is at least $q_*>2t_j\ge r_j$; hence any successful backwards crossing has $\tau_j<\tau_{j-1}$.

<a id="fixed-eq:filtration"></a>
The information used in these choices is central to the proof. Define $$\mathcal W=\sigma(J,x_0,\ldots,x_m,\text{ all bank lists}),\qquad
 \mathcal F_0=\mathcal W,\qquad
 \mathcal F_j=\mathcal W\vee\sigma(r_1,\ldots,r_j).$$ Induction through the explicit rules above gives $$\label{fixed-eq:filtration}
 v_j\text{ is }\mathcal F_{j-1}\text{-measurable},\qquad
 \tau_j\text{ and the active flag after level }j
       \text{ are }\mathcal F_j\text{-measurable}.$$ In particular, neither the selection of $v_j$ nor any default for it uses $r_j$ or a later threshold.

Let $C_1$ denote successful selection of the first reference. On $C_1$ define $s_1=\operatorname{sgn}R(v_1,x_0)\in\{-1,1\}$ and let <a id="fixed-eq:sign-passage"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:sign-passage}
 \mathcal S_n=C_1\cap
 \{\text{there exists }0\le k\le m: s_1R(v_1,x_k)\le0\}.$$ Take this event to be false outside $C_1$. Starting from signed overlap at least $q_*$, a sign passage must visit absolute overlap at most $2/n$, because signed overlaps also change by at most $2/n$. For sufficiently large $n$, it therefore forces the first crossing in <a href="#fixed-eq:first-crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:first-crossing">[fixed-eq:first-crossing]</a>.

<a id="stmt-fixed-lem-nested-crossings"></a>
<div id="fixed-lem:nested-crossings" class="lemma">

<a id="fixed-eq:nested-shells"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Deterministic nesting). *For all sufficiently large $n$, on $\mathcal G_n\cap\mathcal S_n$ every reference selection and every crossing succeeds. For every $1\le j\le p$, $$\label{fixed-eq:nested-shells}
 |D_{v_j}(\tau_j)-r_j|\le2/n,\qquad
 |D_{v_i}(\tau_j)-r_i|\le6j\delta_n
                  \quad(1\le i\le j).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fixed-lem-nested-crossings"><summary>Proof of stmt-fixed-lem-nested-crossings</summary>

*Proof.* At level $1$, simultaneous coverage ensures successful selection and $\mathcal S_n$ ensures a crossing. The initial depth exceeds $r_1$. Minimality of $\tau_1$ and <a href="#fixed-eq:one-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:one-step">[fixed-eq:one-step]</a> therefore put its depth in $[r_1-2/n,r_1]$, proving the assertions at that level.

<a id="fixed-eq:reference-depth"></a>
Assume the assertions through level $j-1$, where $j\ge2$. For every $i<j$, $$D_{v_i}(\tau_{j-1})\le2t_i+6(j-1)\delta_n<3t_i$$ for large $n$. Thus the target is in its cell, and simultaneous coverage supplies an entry $v_j$ in that cell with new depth at least $q_*$. Both $D_{v_i}(\tau_{j-1})$ and $|R(v_i,v_j)|$ are at most $3t_i$, which is less than $b_j$ because $3t_i\le3t_{j-1}<b_j$. The new depth at the target is at least $q_*>t_j/2$. Simultaneous locking gives $$\label{fixed-eq:reference-depth}
 \bigl||R(v_i,v_j)|-r_i\bigr|
 \le\bigl|D_{v_i}(\tau_{j-1})-r_i\bigr|+2\delta_n
 \le(6(j-1)+2)\delta_n.$$

We next verify the locking hypotheses throughout the backwards search. At any visited index $k$ where the new depth is at least $t_j/2$ and the old depths are at most $b_j$, locking implies $$D_{v_i}(k)\le |R(v_i,v_j)|+2\delta_n
             \le3t_i+2\delta_n.$$ The gap $b_j-3t_{j-1}$ is a fixed positive number. Hence <a href="#fixed-eq:one-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:one-step">[fixed-eq:one-step]</a> ensures that every old depth remains below $b_j$ at the next backwards step, for all sufficiently large $n$. Before a crossing, the new depth exceeds $r_j\ge t_j$; at the first crossing step it is at least $r_j-2/n\ge t_j/2$. Starting from the target, these observations prove by induction over the backwards steps that locking continues to apply up to and including the first crossing. If no crossing exists, the induction reaches index $0$.

<a id="fixed-eq:old-depths"></a>
Throughout that portion of the search, locking and <a href="#fixed-eq:reference-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:reference-depth">[fixed-eq:reference-depth]</a> give, for $i<j$, $$\label{fixed-eq:old-depths}
 |D_{v_i}(k)-r_i|\le(6(j-1)+4)\delta_n.$$ A crossing must occur: otherwise this inequality at $k=0$ and $i=1$ would imply $$q_*\le D_{v_1}(0)
 \le2t_1+(6(j-1)+4)\delta_n<q_*.$$ At the backwards crossing, the next index towards the target has depth strictly greater than $r_j$. Consequently $D_{v_j}(\tau_j)\in[r_j-2/n,r_j]$ by the one-step bound. Combining this with <a href="#fixed-eq:old-depths" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:old-depths">[fixed-eq:old-depths]</a> proves <a href="#fixed-eq:nested-shells" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:nested-shells">[fixed-eq:nested-shells]</a>. Since $p$, $q_*$, and all $t_i,b_j$ are fixed, one sufficiently large value of $n$ makes every buffer used in the induction valid simultaneously. ◻

</details>

<a id="fixed-subsec:threshold-estimate"></a>
#### Independent thresholds make the final shells unlikely

<a id="fixed-eq:sign-implies-shells"></a>
The deterministic construction has reduced a sign passage to a single time at which all selected reference depths lie in narrow intervals about their thresholds. Define this event, using the references from the construction including its defaults, by <a id="fixed-eq:shell-event"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:shell-event}
 \mathcal A_n=
 \{\text{there exists }0\le k\le m:
       |D_{v_j}(k)-r_j|\le6p\delta_n
       \text{ for every }1\le j\le p\}.$$ Lemma <a href="#fixed-lem:nested-crossings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fixed-lem:nested-crossings">26</a>, applied at $\tau_p$, shows that $$\label{fixed-eq:sign-implies-shells}
 \mathcal G_n\cap\mathcal S_n\subseteq\mathcal A_n.$$

<a id="fixed-eq:simultaneous-shell-bound"></a>
Fix a deterministic $k$ and set $E_{j,k}=\{|D_{v_j}(k)-r_j|\le6p\delta_n\}$. By <a href="#fixed-eq:filtration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:filtration">[fixed-eq:filtration]</a>, its center $D_{v_j}(k)$ is $\mathcal F_{j-1}$-measurable, while $r_j$ is independent of $\mathcal F_{j-1}$ and uniform on an interval of length $t_j$. Thus <a id="fixed-eq:one-threshold-bound"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:one-threshold-bound}
 \mathbb P(E_{j,k}\mid\mathcal F_{j-1})
 \le\min\{1,12p\delta_n/t_j\}.$$ The earlier events $E_{i,k}$, $i<j$, are $\mathcal F_{j-1}$-measurable. Applying conditional expectation successively for $j=p,p-1,\ldots,1$ therefore gives $$\mathbb P\!\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^pE_{j,k}\,\middle|\,\mathcal W\right)
 \le\prod_{j=1}^p\min\{1,12p\delta_n/t_j\}.$$ The centers may depend on earlier thresholds; the displayed filtration is exactly what allows this product estimate. A union bound over the deterministic indices $k=0,\ldots,m$ now yields $$\label{fixed-eq:simultaneous-shell-bound}
 \mathbb P(\mathcal A_n)
 \le(m+1)\prod_{j=1}^p\frac{12p\delta_n}{t_j}
 \le C_{\beta,a}n^{a+1-pe_*}=o(1).$$ The exponent is less than $-1$ by <a href="#fixed-eq:depth-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:depth-count">[fixed-eq:depth-count]</a>.

<a id="fixed-subsec:tv-conclusion"></a>
#### A fast realized initial state forces a sign passage

<details id="proof-stmt-fixed-lem-nested-crossings-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-fixed-lem-nested-crossings</summary>

<a id="fixed-eq:fast-start-sign-bound"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#fixed-thm:polynomial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fixed-thm:polynomial">23</a>.* First consider continuous time and write $$q_n=\mathbb P_{J,\,x_0\sim\pi^J}
       \{d^J(x_0,t_n)\le1/4\}.$$ The first reference is chosen using only $J,x_0$ and bank $1$. Conditional on these variables, the future chain retains its transition law from the realized $x_0$. On $C_1$, define $$A=A(J,x_0,v_1)=\{z\in\{-1,1\}^n:s_1R(v_1,z)\le0\}.$$ Spin reversal preserves $\pi$ and interchanges positive and negative overlaps, so $\pi(A)\ge1/2$. For any realized $J,x_0$ with $d^J(x_0,t_n)\le1/4$, and any bank $1$ for which $C_1$ holds, the definition of total variation gives $$\mathbb P\{x_{N(t_n)}\in A\mid J,x_0,\text{bank }1\}\ge1/4.$$ If $N(t_n)\le m$, this endpoint event implies $\mathcal S_n$. The Poisson process is independent of all the conditioning variables, so the error in <a href="#fixed-eq:poisson-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:poisson-tail">[fixed-eq:poisson-tail]</a> is uniform under this conditioning. Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P(\mathcal S_n)
 &\ge(1/4-\eta_n)
      \mathbb P\{d^J(x_0,t_n)\le1/4,\ C_1\}\notag\\
 &\ge q_n/4-\eta_n-\tfrac14\mathbb P(C_1^c).
 \label{fixed-eq:fast-start-sign-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ The first-bank case of Lemma <a href="#fixed-lem:bank-coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fixed-lem:bank-coverage">24</a> gives $\mathbb P(C_1^c)=o(1)$. Adding the independent thresholds does not change any probability in this comparison.

Combining <a href="#fixed-eq:good-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:good-event">[fixed-eq:good-event]</a>, <a href="#fixed-eq:sign-implies-shells" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:sign-implies-shells">[fixed-eq:sign-implies-shells]</a>, <a href="#fixed-eq:simultaneous-shell-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:simultaneous-shell-bound">[fixed-eq:simultaneous-shell-bound]</a>, and <a href="#fixed-eq:fast-start-sign-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:fast-start-sign-bound">[fixed-eq:fast-start-sign-bound]</a> gives the explicit estimate <a id="fixed-eq:fast-start-final-bound"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:fast-start-final-bound}
 \frac{q_n}{4}
 \le\eta_n+\tfrac14\mathbb P(C_1^c)+\mathbb P(\mathcal G_n^c)
       +C_{\beta,a}n^{a+1-pe_*}=o(1).$$ This proves <a href="#fixed-eq:continuous-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:continuous-conclusion">[fixed-eq:continuous-conclusion]</a>.

For discrete time set $k_n=\lfloor n^a\rfloor\le m$ and $$q_n^{\mathrm{disc}}
 =\mathbb P_{J,\,x_0\sim\pi^J}
       \{d_{\mathrm{disc}}^J(x_0,k_n)\le1/4\}.$$ Use the same stationary discrete path, banks, scales and thresholds. Whenever the realized initial state is in the event defining $q_n^{\mathrm{disc}}$ and $C_1$ holds, total variation now gives $$\mathbb P\{x_{k_n}\in A\mid J,x_0,\text{bank }1\}\ge1/4.$$ This endpoint is among $x_0,\ldots,x_m$, so it forces $\mathcal S_n$ with no clock-tail error. The same argument therefore gives <a id="fixed-eq:discrete-final-bound"></a>$$\label{fixed-eq:discrete-final-bound}
 \frac{q_n^{\mathrm{disc}}}{4}
 \le\tfrac14\mathbb P(C_1^c)+\mathbb P(\mathcal G_n^c)
       +C_{\beta,a}n^{a+1-pe_*}=o(1),$$ proving <a href="#fixed-eq:discrete-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fixed-eq:discrete-conclusion">[fixed-eq:discrete-conclusion]</a> directly.

For each realized finite chain, total variation is nonincreasing in time, and in continuous time it is also continuous. Hence the first conclusion implies $T^J(x_0)>n^a$. In discrete time it implies $T_{\mathrm{disc}}^J(x_0)>\lfloor n^a\rfloor$; this integer inequality gives $T_{\mathrm{disc}}^J(x_0)>n^a$. Finally, the Gibbs mass of each exceptional set lies in $[0,1]$ and has expectation tending to zero. Markov’s inequality makes that mass tend to zero in probability over $J$; conversely, convergence in probability of a bounded mass implies convergence of its expectation. This proves the asserted equivalent formulations. ◻

</details>

```json
  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The three increments in the perturbation. The upper labels are coefficients of the raw field increments in $Y$; $a = 1 − θ$. Horizontal position records local scalar time, not the mass-sorted order of the hierarchy. Below, the raw covariance of $Y_{0}$ with $X_{s}^{x}$, divided by $β^{2}$, is the tent $T(s)$, which vanishes before the endpoint $q$. Conditional on the common field $X_{r}$, the raw covariance of $Y_{1}$ with $X_{s}^{v}$ on $[r,r+h]$, divided by $β^{2}$, is the ramp $S(s)$. The tent isolates the small change in Parisi mass near $r$; the other branch supplies the mixed susceptibility term. Lengths are schematic.",
      "figure_id": "fig:narrow-increments",
      "pdf_page": 38,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:narrow-increments",
          "exact_quote": "The three increments in the perturbation. The upper labels are\ncoefficients of the raw field increments in $Y$; $a=1-\\theta$.\nHorizontal position records local scalar time, not the mass-sorted order\nof the hierarchy. Below, the raw covarian",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.95cm,y=0.83cm,\n  every node/.style={font=\\small},\n  active/.style={line width=1.3pt,blue!65!black},\n  other/.style={line width=1.3pt,red!65!black}]\n  \\node[anchor=west] at (0,5.00) {Increments used in $Y=aY_0+\\theta Y_1$};\n  \\draw[gray] (0,2.9)--(3,2.9);\n  \\draw[active] (1,2.9)--(3,2.9);\n  \\draw[active] (3,2.9)--(3.7,3.6)--(5,3.6);\n  \\draw[gray] (5,3.6)--(7,3.6);\n  \\draw[other] (3,2.9)--(3.7,2.2)--(5,2.2);\n  \\draw[gray] (5,2.2)--(7,2.2);\n  \\fill (3,2.9) circle[radius=1.8pt];\n  \\node[above] at (1.9,2.94) {$a/h$};\n  \\node[above] at (4.35,3.65) {$-a/h$};\n  \\node[below] at (4.35,2.15) {$\\theta/h$};\n  \\node[anchor=west] at (7.15,3.6) {$x,y$ branch};\n  \\node[anchor=west] at (7.15,2.2) {$v$ branch};\n  \\node[anchor=east,align=right] at (0.85,3.50) {common\\\\branch};\n  \\foreach \\x/\\t in {1/{r-h},3/r,5/{r+h},7/q} {\n    \\draw[gray,densely dotted] (\\x,1.5)--(\\x,3.85);\n    \\node[below] at (\\x,1.5) {$\\t$};\n  }\n  \\node[anchor=west,text=gray] at (8.2,1.12) {local scalar time};\n\n  \\node[anchor=west] at (0,0.30) {Tent $T$ on the $x$ path};\n  \\draw[->,gray] (0,-1.65)--(7.25,-1.65);\n  \\draw[active] (0,-1.65)--(1,-1.65)--(3,-0.65)\n                       --(5,-1.65)--(7,-1.65);\n  \\node[above] at (3,-0.65) {$1$};\n  \\foreach \\x/\\t in {1/{r-h},3/r,5/{r+h},7/q} {\n    \\draw[gray] (\\x,-1.60)--(\\x,-1.70);\n    \\node[below] at (\\x,-1.72) {$\\t$};\n  }\n\n  \\node[anchor=west] at (8.05,0.30) {Ramp $S$ on the $v$ path};\n  \\draw[->,gray] (8.1,-1.65)--(12.65,-1.65);\n  \\draw[other] (8.5,-1.65)--(11.9,-0.65);\n  \\node[above] at (11.9,-0.65) {$1$};\n  \\foreach \\x/\\t in {8.5/r,11.9/{r+h}} {\n    \\draw[gray] (\\x,-1.60)--(\\x,-1.70);\n    \\node[below] at (\\x,-1.72) {$\\t$};\n  }\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A schematic illustration divided into an upper tree-branching diagram and two lower coordinate plots.\n- Upper diagram: Titled \"Increments used in $Y = aY_0 + \\theta Y_1$\". A horizontal line representing the \"common branch\" extends from the left to a branching point at coordinate $r$. At $r$, it bifurcates into two parallel horizontal paths extending rightward: an upper path labeled \"$x, y\\text{ branch}$\" and a lower path labeled \"$v\\text{ branch}$\". A horizontal coordinate axis below marks values $r - h$, $r$, $r + h$, and $q$, labeled \"local scalar time\". The interval $[r - h, r + h]$ along the $x, y$ branch is shaded in light blue, with label \"$a/h$\" above the sub-interval $[r - h, r]$ and label \"$-a/h$\" above $[r, r + h]$. The interval $[r, r + h]$ along the $v$ branch is shaded in light blue and labeled \"$\\theta/h$\".\n- Lower-left plot: Titled \"Tent $T$ on the $x$ path\". A horizontal axis has ticks at $r - h$, $r$, $r + h$, and $q$. A vertical tick marks height 1 at $r$. A piecewise-linear red curve forms a symmetric triangle (tent) starting at 0 at $r - h$, peaking at height 1 at $r$, and returning to 0 at $r + h$, remaining flat at 0 outside this interval.\n- Lower-right plot: Titled \"Ramp $S$ on the $v$ path\". A horizontal axis has ticks at $r$ and $r + h$. A vertical tick marks height 1 at $r + h$. A red line segment starts at 0 at $r$ and ramps linearly up to height 1 at $r + h$."
    },
    {
      "caption": "Why a reset preserves the first reference. The dots are schematic slots in the original level order; omitted entries are indicated by ellipsis, and crossed gray slots represent deletions at earlier resets. Immediately before a reset at level $j$, the preceding block ${j − B, …, j − 1}$ is complete. Conflicts form a suffix of the retained list of length $d ≤ H_{0}",
      "figure_id": "fig:protected-block",
      "pdf_page": 48,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:protected-block",
          "exact_quote": "Why a reset preserves the first reference. The dots are schematic\nslots in the original level order; omitted entries are indicated by\nellipsis, and crossed gray slots represent deletions at earlier resets.\nImmediately before a reset at leve",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.93cm,y=1cm,\n  every node/.style={font=\\small},\n  kept/.style={circle,draw=blue!65!black,fill=blue!65!black,\n               inner sep=0pt,minimum size=4.5pt},\n  conflict/.style={circle,draw=red!70!black,fill=red!70!black,\n                   inner sep=0pt,minimum size=4.5pt}]\n  \\node[align=center] at (3.35,3.45) {Older blocks\\\\with earlier deletions};\n  \\node[align=center] at (9.4,3.45)\n      {Immediately preceding block\\\\all $B$ references still retained};\n  \\node[align=center] at (14.2,3.45) {New\\\\level $j$};\n  \\draw[gray] (6.6,2.96)--(6.6,3.08)--(12.5,3.08)--(12.5,2.96);\n  \\draw[gray,dashed] (13.0,-0.50)--(13.0,3.10);\n  \\node[anchor=east,align=right] at (0.7,2.30) {Before\\\\reset};\n  \\node[anchor=east,align=right] at (0.7,0.15) {After\\\\reset};\n\n  \\foreach \\y in {2.30,0.15} {\n    \\foreach \\x in {1.3,2.1,3.7,5.3}\n      \\node[kept] at (\\x,\\y) {};\n    \\foreach \\x in {2.9,4.5} {\n      \\draw[gray] (\\x,\\y) circle[radius=0.09];\n      \\draw[gray] (\\x-0.08,\\y-0.08)--(\\x+0.08,\\y+0.08);\n    }\n    \\node at (5.95,\\y) {$\\cdots$};\n  }\n  \\node[above] at sep=2pt] at (7.8,-0.80) {Original level order};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A schematic sequence diagram comparing two rows labeled \"Before reset\" and \"After reset\" aligned along a bottom horizontal arrow labeled \"Original level order\".\nThree column headers organize the diagram from left to right:\n1. \"Older blocks with earlier deletions\"\n2. \"Immediately preceding block all $B$ references still retained\"\n3. \"New level $j$\"\n\nIn the \"Before reset\" row:\n- Under \"Older blocks\", there is a solid blue dot labeled $v_1$, followed by blue dots, an ellipsis ($\\dots$), and further blue dots.\n- Under \"Immediately preceding block\", entries begin with a blue dot labeled $v_{j-B}$, followed by blue dots, an ellipsis ($\\dots$), and several dots. A red horizontal bracket spans the final dots in this block, labeled \"Conflict suffix $d \\le H_0 < B$\", with the last dot in the block labeled $v_{j-1}$.\n\nIn the \"After reset\" row:\n- Under \"Older blocks\", the solid blue dot $v_1$, subsequent blue dots, ellipsis ($\\dots$), and blue dots remain unchanged.\n- Under \"Immediately preceding block\", the non-conflicting blue dots are retained. The slots corresponding to the red conflict suffix above are shown as crossed circle symbols ($\\oslash$) indicating deletion, with a vertical dashed arrow pointing down from the conflict suffix to them.\n- Under \"New level $j$\", a vertical arrow labeled \"Select and append\" points down to a newly appended solid blue dot labeled $v_j$."
```
